## Lesson 6 - Barabar and Nagarjuni: the first excavated architecture

Progress: 6/18 | Stage: Core | Subtopic: the oldest known rock-cut architecture in India; Jehanabad district, Bihar; four caves in the Barabar hills and three in the Nagarjuni hills; granite, two chambers, polished interiors and echo effects; the Ajivika dedications by Ashoka and Dasharatha and the titles used; the Lomas Rishi facade as a translation of a thatched hut into stone; the unfinished cave and its missing inscription; later reuse by a Maukhari ruler and a Gupta-period visitor; and the absence of mural painting

━━━ PRE-TEACH CHECKLIST ━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━
Book context: queried - the core account's Mauryan cave architecture section and its Barabar and Nagarjuni passage, which record the district, the date, the patrons, the cave counts, the granite fabric and polish, the Ajivika dedications, the named caves of both hills, the Lomas Rishi facade and its incompleteness, the Karan Chaupar and Vishwakarma details, the absence of murals and Huein Tsang's visit
CA search: not sought - the single current linkage in this session sits in Lesson 14 and is limited there
CA found: none carried for this lesson
━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━

### Visual: the first excavation, and what it copies

```text
   BARABAR HILLS (4 caves)            NAGARJUNI HILLS (3 caves)
   Jehanabad district, BIHAR          nearby; cut a few decades later
   ------------------------------     ------------------------------
   Lomas Rishi                        Gopika
   Sudama                             Vadathi
   Karan Chaupar                      Vapiya
   Vishwakarma (Viswa Mitra)
   ------------------------------     ------------------------------
   dedicatory inscriptions in         inscriptions in the name of
   the name of "KING PIYADASSI"       "DEVANAMPIYA DASARATHA"
   = ASHOKA                           = Ashoka's GRANDSON
   ------------------------------     ------------------------------
              BOTH GROUPS DEDICATED TO THE AJIVIKA SECT
              (contemporary to Jainism and Buddhism)

   THE FORM PROBLEM SOLVED AT LOMAS RISHI
   --------------------------------------
        a wood-and-thatch hut                  the same hut, in granite
        ______                                      ______
       /      \   curved thatched roof            /      \  carved curved
      /        \  on a timber frame     ==>      /        \  "cornice", with
     |  o    o  |                               | ELEPHANTS |  elephants moving
     |__________|                               |__________|  toward stupa
                                                              emblems
   ⚠️ nothing structural is being copied - only the APPEARANCE of a
      timber prototype, cut out of solid rock
```

⚠️ The lower half of that diagram is the conceptual core of the lesson, and of a great deal of Indian architectural history. The first excavators did not invent a cave aesthetic; they reproduced in stone the buildings they already knew, which were made of wood and thatch.

### 1. The problem the first excavators faced

Start from the physical situation. ⚠️ A builder working in timber assembles: posts, beams, rafters, thatch. An excavator working in rock **subtracts**: everything that will be space must be removed, and everything removed is gone permanently. There is no error correction, no adjustment after the fact, and no way to add a member that was cut away by mistake. ⚠️ The first Indian excavators solved this the way most traditions solve a new medium - by imitating the familiar object.

✅ That is exactly what the record shows. In the **Lomas Rishi Cave**, the arch-like facade is a perfect recreation of the monks' wood-and-thatch cottages; on the doorway, carved elephants proceed in a row along the curved cornice towards stupa emblems. ✅ Both the **Lomas Rishi** and **Sudama** caves were clearly modelled on wooden architectural prototypes, and the development of early cave architecture in India can be traced from these two caves in the Barabar hills as its early reference point.

⚠️ Hold that idea, because it recurs across a thousand years. The vaulted chaitya hall with its ribbed ceiling, and Ellora's Cave 10 whose rock is finished to look like wooden beams, are the same phenomenon at later dates. ⚠️ Rock-cut architecture is, for much of its history, carpentry performed by subtraction.

### 2. The facts, stated exactly

✅ The Barabar and Nagarjuni caves are a group of ancient rock-cut caves located in the **Jehanabad district of Bihar**, and are the **oldest known examples of rock-cut architecture in India**, dating back to the **3rd century BC**, during the reign of **Ashoka** and his grandson **Dasharatha** of the Mauryan Empire. ✅ In the Barabar hills there are **four** caves; in the nearby Nagarjuni hills there are **three**. ✅ The caves are **simple in plan**, with **plain but highly polished interiors**.

✅ They were originally created for the use of ascetics from the **Ajivika sect**, which was contemporary to Jainism and Buddhism. ✅ **Three cave inscriptions of Ashoka's time** have been found in the Barabar hills, recording Ashoka's dedication of some caves to Ajivika ascetics; in the Nagarjuni hills, the inscriptions record the dedication of three caves to Ajivikas by his grandson **Dasharatha**. ✅ The rock-cut chambers bear dedicatory inscriptions in the name of **"King Piyadassi"** for the Barabar group and **"Devanampiya Dasaratha"** for the Nagarjuni group.

✅ Most of the caves on the Barabar hills consist of **two chambers**, carved **entirely out of granite**, with a highly polished internal surface and **exciting echo effects**. ✅ **Mural paintings have not been found** in the Barabar and Nagarjuni caves.

✅ The four Barabar caves are **Lomas Rishi, Sudama, Karan Chaupar** and **Vishwakarma**. ✅ **Sudama** consists of two rooms and was dedicated by Ashoka to the Ajivikas. ✅ **Karan Chaupar** consists of a single rectangular room with polished surfaces, and an inscription of the **Gupta dynasty** in the entry chamber reads **"Daridra Kantara"**, the Cave of the Beggars. ✅ The **Vishwakarma** cave, also called Viswa Mitra, is accessible by the **"steps of Ashoka"** carved into the cliff, was offered by Ashoka to the Ajivikas, and is the only cave that does **not** have historical inscriptions after Ashoka. ✅ The three Nagarjuni caves are **Gopika, Vadathi** and **Vapiya**, cut a few decades later than the Barabar group and consecrated by Dasaratha, again for the Ajivika sect. ✅ **Huein Tsang** visited these caves on his journey from Patna to Gaya.

### 3. The single most valuable fact in the topic

⚠️ Of everything in this lesson, one fact does more analytical work than the rest combined: **a Buddhist emperor endowed permanent architecture for a non-Buddhist sect.**

⚠️ Why it matters. The commonest simplification in this entire syllabus area is the equation "Ashoka = Buddhism = Buddhist monuments". ✅ The oldest surviving rock-cut architecture in India refutes it directly: Barabar and Nagarjuni were dedicated to **Ajivika** ascetics, by Ashoka and by Dasharatha, and the inscriptions saying so are in the caves. ⚠️ It follows that Mauryan religious patronage was broader than the emperor's personal affiliation, and that a monument's sectarian user is a separate question from its patron's beliefs.

⚠️ This one fact also does three more jobs in an answer. It is the counter-case that breaks the court-and-popular binary, because the ruling house financed what the classification files under popular art. It is the evidence that inscriptions can settle a question of religious attribution that architecture alone cannot - the chambers themselves are plain, and nothing in their form says "Ajivika". And it is a recorded objective trap: the dedication was by **Ashoka and Dasharatha**, ❌ not by Chandragupta.

### 4. The unfinished cave, and how to use an absence

✅ **Lomas Rishi has no Ashokan inscription**, perhaps because it was never completed, owing to structural rock-slide problems; the other three Barabar caves do have Ashokan inscriptions.

⚠️ This is a small fact with a large methodological lesson. An absence of evidence here has a *recorded physical explanation* attached to it, and that changes how it may be used. ⚠️ Compare it with the absence of an inscription on the Rampurva bull pillar, where the source records the absence and offers no reason at all. ⚠️ In the first case an answer may say "uninscribed, apparently because the excavation was never finished"; in the second it may only say "uninscribed". ⚠️ Learning to feel that difference is worth more than memorising either fact.

### 5. Afterlives: a cave is never used only once

✅ In the Lomas Rishi Cave, **Anantvarman**, a Hindu ruler of the **Maukhari** dynasty, dedicated a statue of Krishna to the cave, according to a Sanskrit inscription on the arch. ✅ At Karan Chaupar, a **Gupta**-period inscription names the cave "Daridra Kantara". ✅ **Huein Tsang** passed this way between Patna and Gaya.

⚠️ Set those three facts side by side and a pattern appears that will recur at Ellora, at Udayagiri-Khandagiri and at Elephanta: an excavated site keeps being re-entered, re-inscribed, renamed and re-dedicated by later people of different religious affiliation. ⚠️ A cave cut for Ajivika ascetics in the third century BC receives a Krishna image from a Hindu ruler centuries later. ⚠️ The accurate general statement is that **sanctity attaches to the place and outlives the sect that first used it** - which is the qualified version of the coexistence argument, and the version that survives scrutiny.

### Examination use

**Where it sits.** ✅ Barabar is the opening evidence in any rock-cut-architecture-as-a-source answer, and it is the standard first example in any chronology of Indian rock-cut architecture.

**Probable framing.** ⚠️ Oldest rock-cut architecture; Mauryan patronage and religion; the origin of cave architecture; wood prototypes in stone.

**Traps.**

1. ❌ Attributing the Barabar dedication to Chandragupta. ✅ Ashoka and Dasharatha, for the Ajivikas.
2. ❌ Calling Barabar Buddhist caves. ✅ They were created for Ajivika ascetics; the account separately notes that early caves used by the Ajivika sect later became popular as Buddhist monasteries.
3. ❌ Describing murals at Barabar. ✅ Mural paintings have not been found there.
4. ❌ Saying Lomas Rishi carries an Ashokan inscription. ✅ It does not, apparently because it was never completed.
5. ⚠️ Treating the wood-prototype facade as structural engineering. ⚠️ It reproduces appearance, not construction.

### Revision notes

1. ✅ Barabar and Nagarjuni: **Jehanabad district, Bihar**; the **oldest known** rock-cut architecture in India; **3rd century BC**.
2. ✅ **Four** caves at Barabar, **three** at Nagarjuni; simple plans; plain but highly polished interiors; **granite**; **echo effects**; **two chambers** in most Barabar caves.
3. ✅ Dedicated to the **Ajivika** sect - by **Ashoka** (Barabar, three inscriptions of his time) and **Dasharatha** (Nagarjuni).
4. ✅ Inscription titles: **"King Piyadassi"** at Barabar; **"Devanampiya Dasaratha"** at Nagarjuni.
5. ✅ Barabar caves: **Lomas Rishi, Sudama, Karan Chaupar, Vishwakarma** (Viswa Mitra). Nagarjuni caves: **Gopika, Vadathi, Vapiya**.
6. ✅ **Lomas Rishi**: facade recreates a wood-and-thatch cottage; elephants along the curved cornice move towards stupa emblems; **no Ashokan inscription**, apparently because it was never completed after rock-slide problems; later Sanskrit inscription of **Anantvarman**, a **Maukhari** ruler, dedicating a Krishna statue.
7. ✅ **Sudama**: two rooms, dedicated by Ashoka to the Ajivikas; with Lomas Rishi, the early reference point for Indian cave architecture; both modelled on wooden prototypes.
8. ✅ **Karan Chaupar**: single rectangular polished room; **Gupta**-period inscription "**Daridra Kantara**".
9. ✅ **Vishwakarma**: reached by the "**steps of Ashoka**"; the only cave with no historical inscriptions after Ashoka.
10. ✅ **No mural paintings** at Barabar or Nagarjuni. ✅ **Huein Tsang** visited, travelling from Patna to Gaya.
11. ⚠️ The decisive analytical fact: a Buddhist emperor endowed permanent architecture for a **non-Buddhist** sect.
12. ⚠️ Sanctity attaches to the place and outlives the sect that first used it.

### Concept check

**Question:** Why is Barabar the strongest single piece of evidence against the sentence "Mauryan religious patronage was Buddhist patronage", and what second conclusion about evidence does the same site support?

**Model answer:** Because the oldest known rock-cut architecture in India was endowed by the Mauryan ruling house itself for a sect that was neither Buddhist nor Jain. The Barabar caves in Jehanabad district, Bihar, dating to the third century BC, were created for ascetics of the Ajivika sect, which was contemporary to Jainism and Buddhism; three cave inscriptions of Ashoka's time record his dedication of caves to Ajivika ascetics, and the nearby Nagarjuni caves record the same dedication to Ajivikas by his grandson Dasharatha, with the inscriptions naming "King Piyadassi" and "Devanampiya Dasaratha" respectively. A ruler's personal affiliation and the sectarian user of the buildings he financed are therefore two different questions. The second conclusion is about evidence itself: the chambers are simple in plan and plain, if highly polished, so nothing in their architecture identifies a sect at all - the religious attribution is known only because the inscriptions are cut into the same rock. Where a cave carries no inscription, its affiliation is an argument from context and comparison, not a fact.

**Misconception to avoid:** Reading the site as evidence of an abstract Mauryan tolerance. What the inscriptions show is that a particular ruling house endowed a particular ascetic community with permanent accommodation; the account separately notes that early caves used by the Ajivikas later became popular as Buddhist monasteries, which is evidence of changing occupancy rather than of a policy of pluralism. The defensible claim concerns patronage practice, not a state philosophy.

## Lesson 7 - The stupa: origin, function, fabric and anatomy

Progress: 7/18 | Stage: Core | Subtopic: the pre-Buddhist origin of the stupa tradition and its non-exclusive significance; the stupa as a relic repository and a votive and commemorative structure; the nine stupas erected after the Buddha's death and their locations; the fabric of unburnt core, burnt outer brick and plaster; the anatomy of anda, medhi, vedika, pradakshina patha, harmika, chhatra and torana, each with its function; vahalkada and kinjalkini; the earliest named examples; and the aniconic caution that governs what may be said about early stupa decoration

### Visual: every part, and the work it does

```text
                             +----+----+   at the SUMMIT of the anda
                        ___________________
                      /                     \

                    /   (hemispherical mound) \   enclosing / commemorating
                   |      relics within        |  the relic
                   |___________________________|
                   |          MEDHI            |  the base / drum on which
                   |___________________________|  the anda stands
              ......|...........................|......
              :     PRADAKSHINA PATHA (path)          :  circumambulatory
              :  |==|==|==|==| VEDIKA |==|==|==|==|   :  passage, defined by
              :        the railing that DEFINES       :  the railing
              :        sacred space and movement      :
              ......................................
                              ||
                          [ TORANA ]   ornamental gateway
                          at Sanchi: FOUR, in the four cardinal
                          directions, each of two pillars with
                          capitals supporting THREE lintels

           -> thick layer of PLASTER; medhi and toran decorated
           with WOODEN sculptures

   ALSO RECORDED AT SANCHI: vahalkada (decorative frontispiece at the
   four cardinal directions, creeper designs, offering flower slabs)
```

⚠️ Read the diagram as a machine rather than a picture. Each named part does something: hold, enclose, honour, define, admit. ⚠️ A candidate who can state the function of each part can answer a question about stupa evolution; a candidate who has memorised the names in order can only label a picture.

### 1. Start with the origin, because it is where the marks are

✅ **The tradition of making stupas is pre-Buddhist**, and stupas **did not have an exclusively Buddhist significance**. ✅ A stupa was generally a **repository of relics**, and it was a **votive and commemorative structure** in the Buddhist tradition.

⚠️ Take those two sentences apart, because they are the single most tested pair of statements in this lesson. The first is about **origin** - the practice of raising such mounds predates Buddhism and was not confined to it. The second is about **function within Buddhism** - a repository, a votive offering, a commemoration. ⚠️ The two are compatible and the distinction between them is what an examiner is testing when the stem offers statements about the "concept of the stupa" alongside statements about what a stupa contained and commemorated.

⚠️ The analytical value of the origin fact extends further. If the form is inherited rather than invented, then what Buddhism did was to *adapt* an existing commemorative practice to a new content - the relics of a teacher and his disciples - and to elaborate it enormously. ⚠️ That is a much more interesting claim than "Buddhists built stupas", and it links directly to the motif evidence in Lesson 9, where pre-Buddhist auspicious figures appear on Buddhist railings.

### 2. The nine stupas, and what they establish

✅ After the death of the Buddha, **nine stupas** were erected. ✅ **Eight** of them had the relics of the Buddha at their **medhi**, while the **ninth** had the **pot** in which the relics were originally kept. ✅ The locations of the nine are **Rajagriha, Vaishali, Kapilavastu, Allakappa, Ramagrama, Vethapida, Pava, Kushinagar** and **Pippalivana**.

⚠️ Three things follow, and all three are usable. ⚠️ First, the relic is the point: the architecture exists because something is inside it, and the division of relics across nine places is a distribution mechanism, turning one death into nine sites of veneration. ⚠️ Second, the ninth stupa contains the *container* rather than the relic, which shows that contact with the relic conferred sanctity on the object - a piece of religious logic that later reappears in the veneration of places, footprints and seats. ⚠️ Third, the placement of relics at the **medhi** is a specific technical fact, and it explains why the medhi is not merely a plinth.

### 3. Fabric: what a stupa is actually made of

✅ The **core** of the stupa was made of **unburnt brick**, while the **outer surface** was made using **burnt bricks**, which were then covered with a **thick layer of plaster**. ✅ The **medhi and the toran were decorated with wooden sculptures**.

⚠️ This is one of the most under-used facts in the topic, and it deserves a paragraph. ⚠️ An unburnt-brick core is cheap, fast and structurally adequate for a solid mound; a burnt-brick skin is durable; plaster gives a finished surface. ⚠️ In other words, the early stupa is an economical structure whose expense is concentrated in its *surface* and its *furniture* - and the furniture, initially, was **wood**.

⚠️ Two conclusions follow. ⚠️ First, the earliest phases of stupa decoration are systematically under-represented in the surviving record, exactly as the Mauryan palace is, because wood does not survive. ⚠️ Second, the later history of the stupa - as the next lesson shows at Sanchi and as Lesson 9 shows in general - is a history of **stone replacing wood** in the railing, the gateway and the relief programme. ✅ The account states that pattern directly for the post-Mauryan period: stupas became larger and more decorative, and stone was increasingly used in place of wood and brick.

### 4. The anatomy, with the function of each part

| Part | ✅ What it is | ⚠️ Function |
|---|---|---|
| **Anda** | The hemispherical mound | Encloses and commemorates the relic; the element that does not change across the whole history of the form |
| **Medhi** | The base or drum supporting the anda | The level at which the relics of eight of the nine original stupas were placed; raises the anda and creates an upper walking level at elaborate sites |
| **Vedika** | The railing | Defines sacred space and produces the circuit along which worshippers move |
| **Pradakshina patha** | The open ambulatory passageway | The path on which devotees walk around the stupa as a token of worship - the ritual act the whole structure is built to make possible |
| **Harmika** | The square railing or enclosure at the summit of the anda | Marks the summit as an enclosed, honoured space |
| **Chhatra / chhatravali** | The umbrella or tiered umbrellas above the harmika | A sign of honour; at Sanchi, a three-tiered umbrella representing the Buddha, the Dhamma and the Sangha |
| **Torana** | The ornamental gateway | Admits and directs; the principal surface for narrative relief |
| **Vahalkada** | Decorative frontispiece at the four cardinal directions | Recorded at Sanchi, with creeper designs and offering flower slabs |
| **Kinjalkini** | A garland of a thousand lotus flowers used in stupa decoration | Symbolises purity and enlightenment |

⚠️ Notice the logic of the whole assembly: a solid mound that cannot be entered, surrounded by a boundary that creates a path, entered through gateways, crowned by markers of honour. ⚠️ **The stupa is not a building one goes into; it is an object one goes around.** That single sentence explains why the vedika and the pradakshina patha matter as much as the anda, and why the chaitya hall of the next lessons places a stupa at the far end of an apsidal space - so that the same circuit can be performed indoors.

### 5. The earliest named examples

✅ Examples include the **Sanchi Stupa** in Madhya Pradesh; **Piprahwa stupa** in Uttar Pradesh, which is the **oldest one**; and **Deur Kothar** in Madhya Pradesh, known for its Buddhist stupas and credited to Ashoka, whose pillar carries the six-line Brahmi inscription believed to be the earliest evidence of the historic Buddha.

⚠️ Keep "Piprahwa - oldest" as a discrete retrieval item. It is exactly the kind of superlative that objective questions are built from, and it is easy to lose because Sanchi dominates attention.

### Criticism and reply: is "pre-Buddhist origin" an over-claim?

⚠️ **The criticism.** A candidate may reasonably ask how much weight the origin claim can bear, given that every early stupa that survives in a developed form is Buddhist.

⚠️ **The reply.** The claim is about the *tradition* and its *significance*, not about the surviving monuments. ✅ The source states both that the tradition of making stupas is pre-Buddhist and that stupas did not have an exclusively Buddhist significance - and the topic's later evidence supports the general pattern, since Lesson 9's motifs show pre-Buddhist auspicious figures being incorporated into Buddhist railing programmes, which is what one expects when a new tradition adapts an existing practice rather than inventing one. ⚠️ What the claim does **not** license is treating any particular mound as non-Buddhist without evidence, or writing that the stupa "was originally a Hindu form", which no source here states.

### Examination use

**Where it sits.** ✅ Stupa anatomy is the most reliably examined vocabulary set in this topic, and the origin-and-function pair is a recurring objective demand.

**Probable framing.** ⚠️ Statement items about the concept, origin and function of the stupa; a part-and-function matching item; and the Mains demand on the evolution of the stupa, which requires anatomy plus accretion at a named site.

**Traps.**

1. ❌ Stating that the stupa concept is Buddhist in origin. ✅ The tradition is pre-Buddhist and its significance was not exclusively Buddhist.
2. ❌ Confusing harmika with vedika. ✅ Harmika is at the summit; vedika is the railing at ground level defining the circuit.
3. ❌ Treating the chhatra as structural. ⚠️ It is a sign of honour.
4. ❌ Inventing stupa dimensions. ⚠️ The only measurement supported in this session is Amaravati's, and it carries the source's own hedge.
5. ⚠️ Forgetting the fabric: unburnt core, burnt skin, plaster, wooden sculpture on medhi and toran.

### Revision notes

1. ✅ The stupa tradition is **pre-Buddhist**; stupas **did not have an exclusively Buddhist significance**.
2. ✅ Function: generally a **repository of relics**; a **votive and commemorative** structure in the Buddhist tradition.
3. ✅ **Nine** stupas after the Buddha's death; **eight** held relics at the **medhi**, the **ninth** held the **pot** in which the relics were kept.
4. ✅ Locations: Rajagriha, Vaishali, Kapilavastu, Allakappa, Ramagrama, Vethapida, Pava, Kushinagar, Pippalivana.
5. ✅ Fabric: **unburnt brick core**, **burnt brick outer surface**, **thick plaster**; **wooden sculptures** on medhi and toran.
6. ✅ Parts and functions: anda (mound enclosing the relic), medhi (base), vedika (railing defining sacred space), pradakshina patha (circumambulatory passage), harmika (square railing at the summit), chhatra/chhatravali (umbrella of honour), torana (ornamental gateway).
7. ✅ At Sanchi, the chattra above the harmika is **three-tiered**, representing **Buddha, Dhamma and Sangha**.
8. ✅ **Vahalkada** = frontispiece at the four cardinal directions with creeper designs and offering flower slabs; **kinjalkini** = a garland of a thousand lotus flowers symbolising purity and enlightenment.
9. ✅ **Piprahwa** (Uttar Pradesh) is the **oldest**; **Deur Kothar** (Madhya Pradesh) is credited to Ashoka.
10. ⚠️ The stupa is an object one walks **around**, not a building one enters - which is why railing and path matter as much as mound.
11. ⚠️ Early decoration was wooden and is therefore under-represented; the later history of the form is stone replacing wood.

### Concept check

**Question:** Explain how the fabric of an early stupa - unburnt core, burnt skin, plaster, wooden furniture - shapes what historians can and cannot see of its earliest phase, and connect that to a claim about the form's later development.

**Model answer:** The fabric concentrates durability in exactly the wrong places for the historian. An unburnt-brick core is a cheap solid mass that survives as a mound but carries no information; the burnt-brick outer surface and its thick plaster are the finished skin; and the parts that carried meaning in the earliest phase - the sculptures on the medhi and on the toran - were wooden, so they have gone. The consequence is that the earliest stupa decoration is systematically invisible, and the surviving record begins, in effect, at the moment when patrons became rich enough to render railing, gateway and relief in stone. That is precisely what the later development consists of: the account records that in the post-Mauryan period stupas became larger and more decorative and stone was increasingly used in place of wood and brick, with the Shunga dynasty introducing the idea of decorated gateways. So the apparent explosion of stupa art after the Mauryas is partly a real increase in expenditure and elaboration and partly a change in the survivability of the medium, and an answer that says both is more accurate than one that says either.

**Misconception to avoid:** Treating the stupa's development as a purely stylistic story. The decisive variables are material and expenditure: what a patron could afford to render permanently determines what later historians can see, so a claim about "the rise of stupa art" always contains a hidden claim about the survival of materials.

## Lesson 8 - Sanchi: one mound as a record of four political orders

Progress: 8/18 | Stage: Core | Subtopic: Sanchi as Kakanaya and its span from the 3rd century BC to the 6th century AD; the Mauryan brick core and the Devi connection with Vidisha; the Sunga doubling, stone casing, vedika, harmika and three-tiered chattra; the Satavahana toranas and the Siri-Satakarni lintel inscription; the Gupta victory inscription and Temple No. 17; Stupas 2 and 3 and the relics of Sariputra and Mahamoggallana; the Ashokan lion-capital pillar with the Schism Edict and the undeciphered Sankha Lipi; the rediscovery, the Begums' patronage and Marshall's restoration; and the 1989 inscription

### Visual: the accretion rail

```text
 STAGE 1  MAURYAN (Ashoka)            STAGE 2  SUNGA
 ----------------------------         ----------------------------
 * brick core of the largest          * brick structure EXPANDED to
   stupa (Stupa no. 1, the              TWICE its size
   Great Stupa)                       * covered with SANDSTONE SLABS
 * nucleus = a simple                 * added: pradakshina path,
   hemispherical BRICK structure        stone railing (VEDIKA),
 * crowned by a CHATTRA to              HARMIKA atop the stupa
   honour and shelter the relics      * above the harmika a THREE-TIERED
 * Ashoka's wife Devi was the           chattra = Buddha, Dhamma, Sangha
   daughter of a merchant of the      * Stupas No. 2 and No. 3 appear to
   nearby ancient town VIDISHA          be commissioned in this period
              |                                     |
              v                                     v
 STAGE 3  SATAVAHANA                  STAGE 4  GUPTA
 (1st c. BC - 2nd c. AD)              (4th c. AD)
 ----------------------------         ----------------------------
 * FOUR stone gateways (TORANAS)      * VICTORY INSCRIPTION of
   in the four cardinal directions      Chandragupta II added on the
   - South, North, East, West           railings of the Great Stupa
 * each gateway: two pillars with     * TEMPLE NO. 17 built in the same
   capitals supporting THREE            premises - earliest phase of
   richly adorned lintels               temple architecture, FLAT ROOF
 * carvings: scenes from the life     * a Gupta-period inscription in
   of the Buddha and JATAKA tales       ornamental SANKHA LIPI on the
 * Siri-Satakarni inscription           Ashokan pillar - STILL UNDECIPHERED
   (Brahmi) on the torana records
   the gift of ONE TOP LINTEL of
   the SOUTHERN torana by the
   ARTISANS of Satakarni II
              |                                     |
              +------------------+------------------+
                                 v
     ONE MOUND = FOUR POLITICAL ORDERS, SEPARABLE BY WHAT EACH ADDED
     ALSO ON SITE: an Ashokan LION CAPITAL pillar (c. 250 BC), flatter
     abacus with geese and flame palmettes, carrying the SCHISM EDICT
```

⚠️ This rail is the most reusable diagram in the session. The method it teaches - identify each phase by *what it specifically added* - transfers to every multi-period monument on the syllabus.

### 1. The learner's problem: a famous site that is usually described, not analysed

Ask most candidates about Sanchi and you get the Great Stupa, four gateways and 1989. ⚠️ All correct, all unanalysed. The question that makes Sanchi valuable is different: *how do we know which part belongs to which period, and what does the sequence prove?*

⚠️ The answer is that the parts are physically separable. A brick core is not a sandstone casing; a railing is not a gateway; an inscription added to a railing centuries later sits on a surface that already existed. ⚠️ Accretion at Sanchi is therefore not an interpretation imposed on the monument - it is a reading of its fabric, corroborated by the inscriptions it carries.

### 2. The site, and its rediscovery

✅ Sanchi, known in ancient times as **Kakanaya**, is presently in **Madhya Pradesh** and has the distinction of holding remarkable specimens of Buddhist art and architecture mainly from the **3rd century BC to the 6th century AD**. ✅ The remains of the Sanchi hillside include many stupas, shrines and monasteries.

✅ The monuments remained deserted for a long period until, in **1851**, the archaeologist **Alexander Cunningham** and **Frederick Charles Maisey** made the first formal survey and excavations at Sanchi and the surrounding stupas of the region. ✅ The ruler of Bhopal at that time, **Shahjehan Begum**, and her successor **Sultan Jehan Begum**, provided money and made efforts for the preservation of the ancient site. ✅ Between **1912 and 1919**, the structures were restored under the supervision of the English archaeologist and Director of the Archaeological Survey of India, **Sir John Marshall**.

⚠️ That short sequence carries an analytical point worth one sentence in an answer: the survival of Sanchi in its present form is the product of nineteenth- and twentieth-century survey, princely-state funding and departmental restoration. ⚠️ What a visitor sees is therefore a *conserved* monument, and an answer that speaks of Sanchi's four gateways is speaking, in part, about the outcome of a restoration programme.

### 3. Stage by stage, with the evidence for each

✅ **Mauryan.** The **brick core** of the largest stupa - **Stupa no. 01**, the **Great Stupa** - was built during the reign of **Ashoka**, whose wife **Devi** was the daughter of a merchant of the nearby ancient town of **Vidisha**. ✅ The nucleus was a **simple hemispherical brick structure**, crowned by a **chatra** intended to honour and shelter the relics.

⚠️ Two details earn marks here. ⚠️ The **Devi-Vidisha** connection supplies a non-doctrinal reason for the site's early importance: a royal marriage into a mercantile family of a nearby town. ⚠️ And the Mauryan phase is **brick**, which matters because everything visible today in stone is later.

✅ **Sunga.** Under the succeeding **Sunga** dynasty, the brick structure was **expanded to twice its size** and **covered with sandstone slabs**. ✅ A **circumambulatory path**, a **stone railing (vedika)** and the **harmika**, a square railing atop the stupa, were added. ✅ Above the harmika, the **chattra**, a **three-tiered umbrella**, was installed, representing the **three Jewels of Buddhism: Buddha, Dhamma and Sangha**. ✅ The other two stupas in the premises, **Stupa No. 02** and **Stupa No. 03**, seem to have been commissioned during Sunga rule; the relics of the great disciples **Sariputra** and **Mahamoggallana** are said to have been placed in **Stupa No. 3**, and Cunningham mentioned these relics in his book **"The Bhilsa Topes"**, which was an attempt to trace Buddhist history through its architectural remains.

⚠️ Notice what this phase actually is: the conversion of a brick mound into a **stone monument with a defined ritual circuit**. The vedika and the path are not decoration; they create the walking space the previous lesson identified as the point of the whole form.

✅ **Satavahana.** The **Satavahana** dynasty, between the **1st century BC and the 2nd century AD**, added **four stone gateways or toranas** to the Great Stupa, positioned in the four cardinal directions - South, North, East and West. ✅ Each gateway features **two pillars with capitals supporting three richly adorned lintels**, and the carvings depict **scenes from the life of the Buddha and the Jataka tales**. ✅ Other decorative and architectural features include the **vahalkada**, a frontispiece at the four cardinal directions with creeper designs and offering flower slabs, and the **kinjalkini**, a garland of a thousand lotus flowers symbolising purity and enlightenment. ✅ The **Siri-Satakarni inscription**, in **Brahmi**, found on the torana, records the **gift of one of the top lintels of the Southern torana by the artisans of the Satavahana king Satakarni II**. ✅ On the torana of the Great Stupa, a decorative yakshi motif of **Salabhanjika** is found; the motif symbolises fertility, auspiciousness and the blossoming of spring, is found in Hindu, Buddhist and Jain art, and appears as bracket figures on pillars and gateways.

⚠️ **This is the single most important attribution caution in the topic.** The inscription records a gift by the **artisans of** the king - a craft group - of **one top lintel** of **one** gateway. ❌ It does not license the sentence "the Satavahana king Satakarni II built the gateways of Sanchi". ⚠️ The correct formulation is "Satavahana-period", with the Siri-Satakarni inscription cited as evidence of the donor world - guilds, artisans and individuals - rather than of a single royal commission.

✅ **Gupta.** During the **Gupta period, in the 4th century AD**, a **victory inscription of Chandragupta II** was added on the railings of the Great Stupa. ✅ **Temple No. 17** was also constructed inside the same premises in the Gupta period and is considered an example of the earliest phase of temple architecture, having a **flat roof** as a common feature.

⚠️ Temple 17 is a hinge between topics. It is the earliest-stage flat-roofed square shrine that opens the temple-architecture sequence, and it stands inside a Buddhist complex - which is itself a fact about how sacred sites accumulated.

### 4. The pillar on the same hill

✅ In the Sanchi premises there is also a **Lion Capital pillar of finely polished sandstone**, erected by Ashoka and very similar to the Lion Capital pillar of Sarnath, but with a **flatter abacus showing alternating pairs of geese and flame palmettes**. ✅ Dated around **250 BC**, the pillar contains **two sets of inscriptions**: **Ashoka's Schism Edict**, which warns of punishment for dissent in the sangha, and a Gupta-period inscription in the ornamental **Sankha Lipi**. ✅ **James Prinsep** deciphered the Brahmi script on Ashokan pillars; the **Sankha Lipi**, a conch-shaped script, **has not yet been deciphered**. ✅ Schism Edicts were found inscribed on the pillars at **Sanchi, Sarnath and Allahabad** (originally in Kaushambi).

⚠️ Put the pillar and the stupa together and Sanchi becomes the topic's best single answer to the question *what did the Mauryan state do with a sacred place?* ⚠️ It endowed a relic monument, and on the same hill it set a polished monolith carrying an instruction about discipline inside the religious order. ⚠️ Art and administration are present in one landscape, and the later dynasties added to both.

### 5. Status, and one modern fact

✅ In **1989**, the **Buddhist Monuments at Sanchi** were declared a **UNESCO World Heritage Site**. ✅ The Sanchi Stupa has been depicted on the Indian currency note of ₹200.

⚠️ The inscription year is the correct current-status anchor for an answer; anything beyond it - management arrangements, monument counts, conservation programmes - belongs to the heritage-institutions topic.

### Comparative note: Sanchi against a single-period monument

⚠️ Compare Sanchi with Barabar. ✅ Barabar is a third-century BC excavation whose later history consists of *re-entries*: a Maukhari ruler's Krishna dedication, a Gupta-period name, a Chinese traveller passing through. ✅ Sanchi is a monument whose later history consists of *additions*: doubling, casing, railing, gateways, inscriptions, a temple.

⚠️ The distinction is worth stating because it generates two different arguments. ⚠️ Barabar demonstrates **continuity of sanctity** across sects - the same rock, re-used by people who believed different things. ⚠️ Sanchi demonstrates **continuity of tradition** across dynasties - the same Buddhist establishment sustained by four politically distinct regimes. ⚠️ Answers that merge the two produce the vague sentence about Indian tolerance; answers that separate them produce two precise claims with different evidence.

### Examination use

**Where it sits.** ✅ Sanchi is the standard named site for the evolution-of-the-stupa demand, the accretion argument, and any question on monuments as stratified evidence.

**Probable framing.** ⚠️ Discuss the evolution of the stupa; rock-cut and structural monuments as historical sources; a statement item on who contributed to the preservation of Sanchi; a pairing item on the site and its features.

**Traps.**

1. ❌ Attributing the toranas to a named Satavahana king. ✅ "Satavahana-period"; the inscription records artisans gifting one lintel of the southern gateway.
2. ❌ Calling the Mauryan phase a stone stupa. ✅ The Mauryan nucleus is brick; the stone casing is Sunga.
3. ❌ Forgetting that Stupas 2 and 3 exist, and that Stupa 3 is associated with the relics of Sariputra and Mahamoggallana.
4. ❌ Treating Temple 17 as a Buddhist stupa. ✅ It is a Gupta-period temple of the earliest flat-roofed stage, inside the same premises.
5. ⚠️ Claiming the Sankha Lipi inscription has been read. ✅ It has not been deciphered.
6. ⚠️ Giving the wrong inscription year. ✅ Sanchi 1989, against Ajanta and Ellora in 1983 and Elephanta in 1987.

### Revision notes

1. ✅ Sanchi, anciently **Kakanaya**, Madhya Pradesh; material mainly from the **3rd century BC to the 6th century AD**.
2. ✅ **Mauryan**: brick core of Stupa no. 1, a simple hemispherical brick nucleus crowned by a chatra; Ashoka's wife **Devi** was the daughter of a merchant of **Vidisha**.
3. ✅ **Sunga**: expanded to **twice its size**; **sandstone slab** casing; pradakshina path, **vedika**, **harmika**; **three-tiered chattra** for **Buddha, Dhamma, Sangha**; Stupas **2** and **3** commissioned; relics of **Sariputra and Mahamoggallana** said to be in **Stupa 3**; Cunningham's **"The Bhilsa Topes"**.
4. ✅ **Satavahana (1st c. BC - 2nd c. AD)**: **four toranas** in the four cardinal directions; each with **two pillars and three lintels**; carvings of the Buddha's life and **Jataka** tales.
5. ✅ **Siri-Satakarni inscription** (Brahmi) on the torana records the gift of **one top lintel of the Southern torana** by the **artisans of Satakarni II** - a donor-world fact, not a royal-commission fact.
6. ✅ **Salabhanjika** yakshi motif on the torana: fertility, auspiciousness, the blossoming of spring; found in Hindu, Buddhist and Jain art.
7. ✅ **Gupta (4th c. AD)**: **victory inscription of Chandragupta II** on the railings; **Temple No. 17**, earliest phase of temple architecture, **flat roof**.
8. ✅ **Ashokan lion-capital pillar** at Sanchi, c. **250 BC**, flatter abacus with geese and flame palmettes; **Schism Edict** plus a Gupta **Sankha Lipi** inscription, **undeciphered**; **Prinsep** deciphered Brahmi.
9. ✅ Rediscovery and conservation: **Cunningham and Maisey, 1851**; **Shahjehan Begum** and **Sultan Jehan Begum** funded preservation; **John Marshall** restored the structures **1912-1919**.
10. ✅ **UNESCO World Heritage Site, 1989**; depicted on the ₹200 note.
11. ⚠️ The method: identify each phase by what it *specifically added*; the phases are separable in the fabric, not merely in the narrative.
12. ⚠️ Sanchi shows continuity of a single tradition across four dynasties; Barabar shows continuity of sanctity across sects. Different claims, different evidence.

### Concept check

**Question:** Using Sanchi alone, show how a single monument can be used as evidence about four political orders, and state the one attribution that a careless answer always gets wrong.

**Model answer:** Each phase left a physically separable contribution, so the monument can be read as a stratified document. The Mauryan phase is a brick core: the nucleus of the Great Stupa was a simple hemispherical brick structure crowned by a chatra, built in Ashoka's reign, with the site's early importance linked to his wife Devi, daughter of a merchant of nearby Vidisha. The Sunga phase converted that mound into a stone monument with a working ritual circuit: the structure was doubled in size and cased in sandstone slabs, and a circumambulatory path, a stone vedika and a harmika were added, with a three-tiered chattra above the harmika representing the Buddha, the Dhamma and the Sangha; Stupas 2 and 3 appear to belong to the same period, with the relics of Sariputra and Mahamoggallana associated with Stupa 3. The Satavahana phase, between the first century BC and the second century AD, added the four toranas in the cardinal directions, each of two pillars carrying three richly carved lintels showing scenes from the Buddha's life and Jataka tales. The Gupta phase added a victory inscription of Chandragupta II on the railings and Temple No. 17, an early flat-roofed shrine, inside the same premises. The attribution a careless answer always gets wrong is the gateways: the Siri-Satakarni inscription records the gift of one top lintel of the southern torana by the artisans of King Satakarni II, which evidences a donor world of craft groups under Satavahana rule, not the construction of four gateways by a named king.

**Misconception to avoid:** Believing that naming the most recent or most famous phase is enough. An answer that says "Sanchi's Great Stupa, with its four Satavahana gateways, was built by Ashoka" has fused three phases into one sentence and lost every distinction the monument exists to demonstrate.

## Lesson 9 - Bharhut, the Shunga moment, and the early narrative phase

Progress: 9/18 | Stage: Core | Subtopic: the post-Mauryan political field; the Shunga introduction of decorated gateways and the shift from wood and brick to stone; Bharhut as a distinct stupa with early narrative relief, yaksha-yakshi figures and aniconic representation of the Buddha; the Bharhut Yavana; the source's approximate chronology of early stupas; Bodhgaya railings; the shared motif vocabulary of Shalabhanjika and Gajalakshmi; and the strict boundary with the sculpture topic

### Visual: three early stupas, three different analytical jobs

```text
                                                                  DISPERSAL history

   THE BOOK'S APPROXIMATE DATES, REORDERED HERE INTO SEQUENCE
   ----------------------------------------------------------------
   Bharhut Stupa .................. 150 BC
   Second Stupa at Sanchi ......... 125 BC

   Great Stupa of Sanchi .......... 50 BC

```

⚠️ The chronology rail is the reason this lesson exists. Without it, an answer can only list early stupas; with it, an answer can say which came first, and comparison becomes possible.

### 1. The political field after the Mauryas, in one paragraph

✅ After the decline of the Mauryan Empire in the **2nd century BC**, small dynasties sprang up in various parts of India: the **Shungas, Kanvas, Kushanas and Shakas** in the north, and the **Satavahanas, Ikshvakus, Abhiras and Vakatakas** in southern and western India. ✅ The religious scene saw the emergence of Brahmanical sects - **Shaivites, Vaishnavites and Shaktites**. ✅ The art of the period began to reflect the changing socio-political scenario; architecture in the form of rock-cut caves and stupas continued, with each dynasty introducing unique features of its own, and different schools of sculpture emerged, with the art of sculpture reaching its climax in the post-Mauryan period.

⚠️ For this topic, the political detail matters only as a patronage map: the loss of a single imperial patron and the rise of several regional ones changes *who* commissions monuments, and therefore what monuments look like. ⚠️ The dynastic narrative itself belongs to ancient history.

### 2. What the Shungas did to the stupa

✅ Stupas became **larger and more decorative** in the post-Mauryan period. ✅ **Stone was increasingly used in place of wood and brick.** ✅ The **Shunga dynasty introduced the idea of torans** as beautifully decorated gateways to the stupas; the torans were intricately carved with figures and patterns, and are described in the account as evidence of **Hellenistic influence**.

⚠️ Two observations, one technical and one cautionary. ⚠️ Technically, this is the completion of the process begun in Lesson 7: the wooden furniture of the early stupa becomes stone, and once it is stone it can carry a sustained relief programme, which is why narrative art appears at scale in this period. ⚠️ Cautiously, the Hellenistic-influence claim is the account's, and it is a claim about carved ornament on gateways; it should be reported with its owner and not expanded into a general thesis about Greek origins of Indian relief.

### 3. Bharhut, stated exactly and no further

✅ The **Bharhut Stupa**, **Madhya Pradesh**, of the **Shunga period**, is listed among the notable post-Mauryan stupas alongside **Sanchi No. 1**, the **Bodhgaya railings** and **Amaravati**, and is characterised by **early narrative reliefs**, **Yaksha-Yakshi figures** and the **aniconic representation of the Buddha**. ✅ The **Bodhgaya railings**, Bihar, of the Shunga period, are recorded as stone railings and pillars with carving including lotus and elephants.

✅ From the sculpture chapter: the Mauryan empire was replaced by the **Shunga dynasty in 185 BCE**; the Buddhist sculpture of the **Bharhut Stupa, in Satna district of Madhya Pradesh**, is the most noted excellence of this period; the **northern gateway** of the Bharhut stupa is the finest example of a work of art there; and the **Bharhut Yavana** is a relief of a warrior dated to **100 BCE**.

⚠️ **A limitation to state plainly.** The list of notable post-Mauryan stupas is incomplete in the text available for this session: the Amaravati entry breaks off mid-description, and the feature descriptions of several entries are cut short. ❌ Nothing is written here beyond what is actually recorded, and no missing text is reconstructed. ⚠️ Stating this openly is not an admission of weakness; it is the reason the Bharhut material in this session stays narrow and exact.

⚠️ **The Yavana relief** deserves one sentence of interpretation and no more. ✅ It is a dated relief of a warrior described by the term Yavana, which was used for Greek speakers in ancient times. ⚠️ It is therefore usable in any cultural-contact demand as evidence that foreign figures were represented in Indian religious art by about 100 BCE. ❌ Do not assign an origin to the individual depicted; "Yavana" is a category term.

### 4. The aniconic point, held at the boundary

✅ Bharhut is characterised by the **aniconic representation of the Buddha** - that is, the Buddha is present in the narrative through symbols rather than through a human figure. ✅ At Nasik, the same convention is recorded for the Hinayana phase: the Buddha's presence is indicated through motifs and symbols such as a **throne** and **footprints**, with images of the Buddha carved later under Mahayana influence.

⚠️ **Where this session stops.** The full account of the movement from symbol to anthropomorphic image - its chronology, its causes, the Gandhara and Mathura schools, the iconographic typology of mudras and postures - belongs to the sculpture and iconography topic. ❌ It is not re-derived here. ⚠️ What this topic owns is the **architectural correlate**: a chaitya hall originally centred on a stupa, and later chaityas and viharas incorporating a Buddha image in a shrine, which is exactly the innovation recorded at Ajanta's second phase in Lesson 13. ❌ And Bharhut may not be used as an example of the anthropomorphic Buddha image, because the source characterises its representation as aniconic.

### 5. Shared motifs, and what they argue

✅ **Shalabhanjika** - a yakshi holding a tree - appears as bracket figures on the gateways of the **Great Stupa at Sanchi** and also at **Bharhut**; it is a pre-Buddhist and non-Buddhist motif incorporated as an auspicious symbol, and it is found in Hindu, Buddhist and Jain art. ✅ A **Gajalakshmi** image occurs on the railings at **Bharhut**, on a 3rd-century AD coin from **Kausambi**, and on the **vedika at Sanchi**.

⚠️ This is the single best evidence in the topic for a claim that is otherwise easy to assert and hard to support: that people who turned to Buddhism enriched it with their own pre-Buddhist practices. ⚠️ The argument runs from objects: the same auspicious figures appear on Buddhist railings and gateways, in Hindu and Jain contexts, and on a coin - so the motif vocabulary was shared across religious boundaries even where doctrine was not. ⚠️ It also reinforces Lesson 7's origin point: a tradition that borrowed the stupa form also borrowed the decorative repertoire that surrounded it.

### Comparative note: why Bharhut is the right comparator for Sanchi

⚠️ Bharhut isolates a variable. ⚠️ Sanchi is a monument of accretion, so any statement about "the art of Sanchi" is a statement about four periods at once. ⚠️ Bharhut, as recorded here, is a **Shunga-period** stupa characterised by narrative relief and aniconic representation - one period's work, available as a control. ⚠️ Amaravati, in the next lesson, adds a third case: elaboration under one dynasty plus a colonial excavation history. ⚠️ Three sites, three analytical jobs, and an answer that uses them in that way is arguing rather than listing.

### Examination use

**Where it sits.** ✅ Bharhut supplies the second named early stupa that an evolution-of-the-stupa answer needs so that it does not rest on one monument, and it supplies the chronology rail.

**Probable framing.** ⚠️ Early Buddhist stupa art and its folk motifs; the evolution of the stupa; cultural contact in early Indian art. ✅ An older direct Mains demand in the core source's reproduced list asks how early Buddhist stupa art, while depicting folk motifs and narratives, successfully expounds Buddhist ideals - which is exactly this lesson's motif evidence plus Lesson 8's gateway programme.

**Traps.**

1. ❌ Using Bharhut as an example of the Buddha image. ✅ Its representation is aniconic.
2. ❌ Converting the book's approximate dates into a precise construction chronology. ⚠️ Report them as the book's own approximate figures.
3. ❌ Adding Bharhut detail beyond what the evidence actually states.
4. ⚠️ Attributing the Shunga gateway idea to the Satavahanas. ✅ The Shungas introduced decorated gateways; the Sanchi toranas are Satavahana-period.
5. ❌ Naming an origin for the individual in the Bharhut Yavana relief.

### Revision notes

1. ✅ Post-Mauryan patrons: **Shungas, Kanvas, Kushanas, Shakas** in the north; **Satavahanas, Ikshvakus, Abhiras, Vakatakas** in the south and west. Brahmanical sects emerge: Shaivites, Vaishnavites, Shaktites.
2. ✅ Post-Mauryan stupas became **larger and more decorative**; **stone replaced wood and brick**; the **Shungas introduced torans**, described as evidence of Hellenistic influence.
3. ✅ **Bharhut** (MP, **Satna district**), **Shunga period**: early **narrative reliefs**, **Yaksha-Yakshi** figures, **aniconic** representation of the Buddha; the **northern gateway** is its finest work.
4. ✅ **Bharhut Yavana**: relief of a warrior, dated **100 BCE**; Yavana = Greek speakers; a category term, not a named origin.
5. ✅ Shunga dynasty replaced the Mauryan empire in **185 BCE**.
6. ✅ The book's approximate dates, reordered here into sequence: **Bharhut 150 BC; Sanchi Second Stupa 125 BC; Bodhgaya 75 BC; Great Stupa of Sanchi 50 BC; Third Sanchi Stupa 25 BC** - cite the values as the book's approximations and the ordering as this session's arrangement.
7. ✅ **Bodhgaya railings** (Bihar, Shunga): stone railings and pillars with lotus and elephant carving.
8. ✅ **Shalabhanjika**: yakshi with a tree; bracket figures at Sanchi and Bharhut; found in Hindu, Buddhist and Jain art. ✅ **Gajalakshmi**: on Bharhut railings, a 3rd-century AD Kausambi coin, and the Sanchi vedika.
9. ⚠️ Shared motif vocabulary is the strongest evidence that converts came with their own auspicious practices.
10. ⚠️ Boundary: the symbol-to-image transition and the sculpture schools belong to the sculpture topic; this topic owns the architectural correlate - stupa-centred hall, later shrine-image.
11. ⚠️ Limitation: the list of notable post-Mauryan stupas is incomplete in the text available for this session; only what is recorded is used.

### Concept check

**Question:** Why does an answer on early Buddhist stupa art need Bharhut even when it already has Sanchi, and what does the pair allow you to say that neither allows alone?

**Model answer:** Because Sanchi is a monument of accretion and therefore cannot function as evidence about a single period. Its fabric runs from a Mauryan brick core through a Sunga stone casing and railing to Satavahana-period gateways and Gupta additions, so a sentence about "the art of Sanchi" silently mixes four periods. Bharhut, by contrast, is recorded as a Shunga-period stupa characterised by early narrative reliefs, yaksha-yakshi figures and the aniconic representation of the Buddha, with its northern gateway noted as its finest work and a dated relief, the Bharhut Yavana of about 100 BCE. Holding the two together allows a claim that neither supports alone: that by the Shunga period a fully developed narrative relief programme existed in which the Buddha was present through symbols rather than as a figure, and that this programme was then carried onto the gateways of a monument whose core was already two centuries old. The shared motifs close the argument, since Shalabhanjika appears as bracket figures at both Sanchi and Bharhut and Gajalakshmi appears on Bharhut's railings and on Sanchi's vedika, showing one decorative vocabulary moving across sites and religions.

**Misconception to avoid:** Treating the aniconic phase as evidence that early Buddhists were forbidden to depict the Buddha, or as a doctrinal claim of any kind. What this topic owns is the observed convention on particular monuments and its architectural consequence; the explanation of the shift from symbol to image belongs to the sculpture and iconography topic and should not be improvised here.

## Lesson 10 - Amaravati: a stupa, a school, an inscriptional corpus and a dispersal

Progress: 10/18 | Stage: Core | Subtopic: location in the lower Krishna valley and the ancient names Dhanyakataka and Dharanikota; the Mahachaitya and its recorded scale; vedika, toranas and the distinctive ayaka pillars with the five events; the Ashokan foundation claim and the Satavahana enlargement; Prakrit-Brahmi inscriptions as social evidence; Mackenzie, Elliot and Burgess and the Amaravati marbles; the Ikshvaku continuation and the related Krishna-valley sites; and the strict boundary with the sculpture topic

### Visual: one site, five different kinds of question

⚠️ Amaravati is the topic's most economical case study because one site answers five different examination demands. A candidate short of time who knows one site thoroughly should know this one.

### 1. The site and its names

✅ The Amaravati Buddhist site is located **near the banks of the Krishna river**, in the town of Amaravati in **Guntur district, Andhra Pradesh**. ✅ It was known in ancient times as **Dhanyakataka** or **Dharanikota**, and was a major centre of **Mahayana** Buddhism, drawing large numbers of scholars and pilgrims. ✅ The site flourished as a major trade centre between the **3rd century BC and the 12th century AD**, with major contributions by the **Satavahana** dynasty, which ruled from the 2nd century BC to the 3rd century AD; the Satavahanas were primarily followers of Brahmanism but were also great patrons of Buddhism as well as Jainism.

⚠️ That last clause is worth pausing on, because it repeats the Barabar lesson in a different register. ⚠️ A dynasty whose rulers followed one tradition financed monuments of another - and the correct inference is about patronage behaviour, not about personal conviction or abstract tolerance. ⚠️ The ancient names are themselves a tested identifier; an item can name Dhanyakataka and expect the candidate to recognise Amaravati.

### 2. The monument

✅ The Amaravati Stupa, known as the **Mahachaitya**, is **believed to be originally 27 metres tall**, with a large circular dome and ornate carvings. ✅ It was surrounded by a stone railing (**vedika**) and **four ornamental gateways (toranas)**, similar to the Sanchi Stupa. ✅ It features **ayaka pillars**, distinctive structures found at the stupa's **four cardinal directions**, **typically five in number on each platform**, believed to represent significant events in the Buddha's life: **birth, renunciation, enlightenment, Dharmachakra Pravartana and Mahaparinirvana**.

⚠️ Note the hedges and keep them. ✅ "Believed to be originally 27 metres tall" is the source's formulation, and this is the **only** stupa or cave measurement used anywhere in this session. ❌ No other dimension is invented for any monument here. ⚠️ Similarly, the ayaka pillars' association with five events is given as a belief, not as an inscriptional statement.

⚠️ The ayaka pillar is the single best form-discriminator in the topic. ⚠️ Sanchi and Amaravati share the vedika and the four gateways; the ayaka platforms are what make an Amaravati-type stupa recognisable, and they are a regional development of the Krishna valley rather than a universal feature.

### 3. Patronage, and the inscriptions

✅ A grand Buddhist stupa, **believed to have been constructed earlier by King Ashoka**, was **enlarged and embellished by the Satavahanas**, with ornate **limestone railings**, sculpted panels, and gateways depicting **Jataka tales**, scenes from the Buddha's life and various Buddhist symbols. ✅ The site has numerous **inscriptions written in Prakrit and in Brahmi script**, describing the **socio-religious life, patronage patterns and linguistic history** of those times.

⚠️ This is the accretion pattern again - an earlier foundation enlarged by a later dynasty - and it is worth noticing that the accretion here is stated with a hedge ("believed to have been constructed earlier") where Sanchi's Mauryan core is stated flatly. ⚠️ An answer that reproduces the difference in confidence is more accurate than one that flattens both into the same assertion.

⚠️ The inscriptions matter for a reason beyond dating. ⚠️ Inscriptions that record **patronage patterns** identify donors - and donors at such sites, as the Sanchi lintel inscription showed, include artisans, guilds and individuals rather than only rulers. ⚠️ This is the evidential basis for the claim in Lesson 18 that the great Buddhist monuments record a broad donor society and not merely royal munificence.

### 4. The afterlife: rediscovery, excavation and dispersal

✅ The Mahachaitya later fell into ruins. ✅ In **1797**, **Colonel Colin Mackenzie** visited the place, leading to the rediscovery of the stupa. ✅ Its **first excavation** was done by **Sir Walter Elliot in 1845**, and he collected what is known as the **Amaravati marbles**, later famous as the **Elliot marbles**, sculptures kept in the **British Museum**. ✅ The archaeologist **James Burgess** visited and recorded findings about the stupa in **1887**. ✅ The **Archaeological Survey of India has conserved the site**, and a **replica** of the stupa has been created near the original location.

⚠️ **The discipline here is exact.** ✅ The facts above - collection, location of the marbles in the British Museum, conservation, replica - are recorded and may be written flatly. ❌ What may not be done is to convert the dispersal into a repatriation claim: any statement about restitution, current negotiations or institutional obligations requires a dated official source naming object, institution and provenance, and belongs to the heritage-institutions topic.

⚠️ Amaravati is therefore the topic's clearest example of a monument whose *historiography* is part of its history. The object a museum visitor sees in London, the ruin an archaeologist recorded in 1887 and the replica standing near the site today are three different things, and only careless writing treats them as one.

### 5. The boundary with the sculpture topic, stated once

✅ Under Satavahana patronage, Amaravati developed as one of the three famous schools of Buddhist sculpture, the **Amaravati school of art**, which the account describes as developed on purely indigenous lines; the **Ikshvaku** dynasty, which came later, also patronised the school in the 3rd-4th centuries AD. ✅ The great stupa was adorned with **greenish-white limestone narrative reliefs** depicting jataka stories and scenes of the Buddha's life, and the style came to be termed **narrative art**; the **tribhanga** posture was used extensively. ✅ A particular depiction of the Buddha from Amaravati, with the robe on the left shoulder and the other hand in **abhayamudra**, became so iconic that it was replicated in other parts of South and South-East Asia. ✅ The school influenced the art of successive South Indian dynasties, and the style is also seen in the stupa remains at **Bhattiprolu, Jaggayyapeta, Nagarjunakonda, Ghantasala and Goli** in the Krishna valley, and as far west as **Ter in Maharashtra**. ✅ **Hiuen Tsang** visited Amaravati in **640 AD** and studied there.

⚠️ **Why this appears here at all.** The sculpture topic owns the school, its stylistic analysis and its comparison with Gandhara and Mathura, and this session does not develop any of that. ⚠️ It is recorded here because a verified objective demand on Amaravati tests the *site* and the *school's reach* in the same item, and a candidate who has studied the stupa without knowing that its sculpture travelled would meet the question with half the material. ⚠️ Beyond this paragraph, the school is handed over.

### Examination use

**Where it sits.** ✅ Amaravati is the named Krishna-valley case for stupa elaboration, for donor-society evidence and for colonial excavation history.

**Probable framing.** ⚠️ Statement items on the stupa's location, scale, school and external reach; a Mains demand on stupa evolution or on Buddhist art centres; a question on archaeologists associated with early Indian sites.

**Traps.**

1. ⚠️ Amaravati is identified by the **Krishna river** and by its ancient names; the gorge identifier in this session's evidence belongs to Ajanta.
2. ❌ Inventing dimensions. ⚠️ Only the "believed to be originally 27 metres" figure is supported, with its hedge.
3. ❌ Turning the Elliot marbles into a repatriation claim.
4. ❌ Attributing the whole monument to the Satavahanas. ✅ An earlier stupa, believed to be Ashokan, was enlarged and embellished by them.
5. ⚠️ Forgetting the ayaka pillars, which are the site's distinctive form-discriminator.

### Revision notes

1. ✅ Amaravati: near the **Krishna river**, **Guntur district, Andhra Pradesh**; anciently **Dhanyakataka / Dharanikota**; a major centre of **Mahayana** Buddhism; a trade centre from the **3rd century BC to the 12th century AD**.
2. ✅ The stupa is called the **Mahachaitya**, **believed to be originally 27 metres tall**, with a large circular dome, a stone **vedika** and **four toranas**, similar to Sanchi.
3. ✅ **Ayaka pillars** at the **four cardinal directions**, **typically five per platform**, believed to represent **birth, renunciation, enlightenment, Dharmachakra Pravartana and Mahaparinirvana**.
4. ✅ A stupa **believed built earlier by Ashoka** was **enlarged and embellished by the Satavahanas** with ornate **limestone** railings, sculpted panels and gateways showing **Jataka** tales and scenes from the Buddha's life.
5. ✅ Numerous inscriptions in **Prakrit**, in **Brahmi** script, recording socio-religious life, patronage patterns and linguistic history.
6. ✅ **Mackenzie 1797** (rediscovery); **Walter Elliot 1845** (first excavation; **Amaravati / Elliot marbles**, in the **British Museum**); **Burgess 1887**; ASI conservation; a **replica** stupa near the original location.
7. ✅ The **Amaravati school** is owned by the sculpture topic; at the boundary, note its indigenous development, Ikshvaku continuation, narrative limestone relief, tribhanga posture, the abhayamudra Buddha replicated in South and South-East Asia, and its influence on later South Indian sculpture.
8. ✅ Related Krishna-valley sites: **Bhattiprolu, Jaggayyapeta, Nagarjunakonda, Ghantasala, Goli**; and **Ter** in Maharashtra. ✅ **Hiuen Tsang** visited in **640 AD**.
9. ⚠️ Amaravati links monument, school, dynasty, epigraphy, colonial excavation and a live dispersal question in one case.
10. ❌ Dispersal is not repatriation: any restitution claim needs a dated official source and belongs to the heritage topic.

### Concept check

**Question:** A single item asks you to judge statements about Amaravati's location, its scale relative to other stupas, and the reach of its sculpture. Which of those three is the most dangerous to answer from memory, and how should each be handled?

**Model answer:** Scale is the dangerous one. Location is secure and precise: the site lies near the Krishna river in Guntur district, Andhra Pradesh, known anciently as Dhanyakataka or Dharanikota, and it is not a gorge site. Reach is also recorded: the Amaravati school developed on indigenous lines, was patronised by the Satavahanas and later the Ikshvakus, influenced the art of successive South Indian dynasties, appears at Bhattiprolu, Jaggayyapeta, Nagarjunakonda, Ghantasala, Goli and as far west as Ter, and produced a depiction of the Buddha with the robe on the left shoulder and the other hand in abhayamudra that was replicated in South and South-East Asia. Scale, by contrast, is carried in the sources only as a hedged statement that the Mahachaitya is believed to have been originally about 27 metres tall; no comparative ranking against other stupas is supported by the material held here. The disciplined handling is to assert location and reach, to state the height only with its hedge, and to treat any comparative size claim as unresolved rather than guessing.

**Misconception to avoid:** Assuming that because a site is famous, every superlative attached to it is safe. Amaravati attracts comparative claims about size and importance that the sources do not underwrite, and an answer that repeats one of them has exchanged a verifiable fact for an impression.
