## Lesson 11 - Chaitya and vihara: function-driven form

Progress: 11/18 | Stage: Core | Subtopic: the two rock-cut types and their functions; the vihara's Mauryan-period origin and the chaitya hall's post-Mauryan development; the apsidal, colonnaded, vaulted chaitya with a stupa at its terminal end against the source's own general description of quadrangular flat-roofed prayer halls; open courtyards and stone screen walls; the later introduction of a shrine room into the vihara; and a recorded description problem stated honestly

━━━ PRE-TEACH CHECKLIST ━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━
Book context: queried - the core account's rock-cut cave architecture section for the post-Mauryan period, which records the development of two types of rock caves, defines the vihara as a residential hall developed during the Mauryan Empire and the chaitya hall as developed in the post-Mauryan period, describes chaitya halls as mainly quadrangular chambers with flat roofs used as prayer halls, and records open courtyards, stone screen walls and human and animal decoration; the standard description of the developed rock-cut chaitya as commonly apsidal with a stupa at the terminal end, colonnades and a vaulted roof; and the Ajanta passage recording the introduction of a shrine room into the vihara as an innovation of that period
CA search: not sought - the single current linkage in this session sits in Lesson 14 and is limited there
CA found: none carried for this lesson
━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━

### Visual: two plans, two purposes

```text
   CHAITYA - a hall for ASSEMBLY around an object of worship
   ---------------------------------------------------------
        entrance
           ||
     ______||______________________________________
    |      ||                                      \
    |  o   o   o   o   o   o   o   o   o            \      <- COLONNADE
    |                                        _____   |        (two rows of
    |            central nave               ( STUPA) |         pillars) makes
    |                                        \_____/ |        an aisle for
    |  o   o   o   o   o   o   o   o   o            /        CIRCUMAMBULATION
    |______________________________________________/
                                             ^
                                    APSIDAL (rounded) END
                                    with the STUPA at the
                                    TERMINAL end of the hall
   roof: VAULTED in the developed type

   VIHARA - a RESIDENCE for monks
   ---------------------------------------------------------
     +--------+--------+--------+--------+--------+
     |  cell  |  cell  |  cell  |  cell  |  cell  |
     +--------+                          +--------+
     |  cell  |      CENTRAL HALL        |  cell  |
     +--------+     (open square)        +--------+
     |  cell  |                          |  cell  |
     +--------+--------+--------+--------+--------+
                  |  veranda / entrance |
                  +---------------------+
       LATER INNOVATION (Ajanta phase II): a SHRINE ROOM
       cut into the rear wall, so the residence acquires an
       image and the two functions begin to combine

   THE RULE:  chaitya = WORSHIP / ASSEMBLY      vihara = RESIDENCE
              ❌ never reverse them
```

⚠️ Everything in this session from here on is read off these two plans. A cave site is a distribution of these two forms, and counting which form dominates is an argument about what the community there was doing.

### 1. The distinction, stated so that it cannot be reversed

✅ The post-Mauryan period saw the development of two types of rock caves - **chaitya** and **vihara**. ✅ **Viharas** were **residential halls** for Buddhist and Jain monks and were **developed during the time of the Mauryan Empire**; the **chaitya halls** were **developed during** the post-Mauryan period. ✅ The caves also had **open courtyards** and **stone screen walls** to shield from rain, and were decorated with **human and animal figures**.

⚠️ The memory device that works is causal rather than verbal. ⚠️ A monastic community needs two things from architecture: somewhere to *live* and somewhere to *gather around the object of veneration*. The residence comes first historically, because shelter is the prior need and because the Mauryan-period caves - Barabar's simple polished chambers - were used as living quarters. The assembly hall develops later, once communities are large, settled and endowed enough to need a congregational space. ⚠️ Hold the sequence and the definitions follow from it.

### 2. The chaitya's developed form, and what each element does

⚠️ The developed rock-cut chaitya is standardly described as a Buddhist prayer or assembly hall, **commonly apsidal** in rock-cut examples, with a **stupa at its terminal end**, **colonnades** and a **vaulted roof**.

⚠️ Take the elements one at a time, because each solves a problem set in Lesson 7.

- ⚠️ **A stupa at the terminal end.** The object of worship is the stupa, exactly as in the open-air monument. The hall exists to place that object indoors.
- ⚠️ **An apsidal - that is, rounded - end.** The curve exists because the ritual is circumambulation. A square end would force worshippers to turn corners; an apse lets the path run continuously around the stupa.
- ⚠️ **Colonnades.** Two rows of pillars divide the hall into a central nave and side aisles, and the aisles *are* the circumambulatory path. The colonnade is not decoration; it is the pradakshina patha translated into an interior.
- ⚠️ **A vaulted roof.** The vault reproduces in rock the curved, ribbed roof of a timber-and-thatch hall - the same translation that Lesson 6 found at the Lomas Rishi facade.

⚠️ So the chaitya hall is the open-air stupa enclosure taken indoors and cut out of a cliff, with the timber prototype still visible in its ceiling. ⚠️ A candidate who can say that has explained a plan rather than described one, which is precisely the difference a 15-mark answer rewards.

### 3. A recorded description problem, stated honestly

⚠️ Two descriptions of the chaitya hall are in circulation, and they do not use the same words. The difference is worth stating rather than smoothing over.

| Description | What it says |
|---|---|
| ✅ The developed excavated type | Chaitya = prayer/assembly hall, **commonly apsidal** in rock-cut examples, **stupa at the terminal end**, **colonnades**, **vaulted roof** |
| ✅ The general definition of the class | Chaitya halls "were mainly **quadrangular chambers with flat roofs** and were used as prayer halls" |

⚠️ Both are in front of the reader, and the honest handling is this. ⚠️ The **function** is identical in both: a hall used for prayer and assembly, as against a residence. ⚠️ The **form description** differs, and the safest position for an answer is to lead with function, then describe the developed rock-cut type - apsidal, colonnaded, vaulted, stupa at the far end - which is the form actually visible at the great western Deccan and Ajanta chaityas, while noting that plan and roof form vary across sites and periods. ❌ What must not be done is to assert a single universal chaitya shape as though no variation existed, or to quietly drop one description because the other is more convenient.

⚠️ This is also a transferable lesson about evidence. Two accounts of the same category can differ because one is describing a general class and the other a developed exemplar. Recording the difference costs one sentence and demonstrates exactly the source-critical habit this topic rewards.

### 4. How the two forms recombine

✅ At Ajanta, the account records that the **introduction of a shrine room into the vihara is an innovation of** the later period, and that viharas there display a profusion of sculptural ornamentation like the chaityas. ✅ At Nasik, the Hinayana-phase symbolic indication of the Buddha through motifs such as a throne and footprints later gives way to carved images of the Buddha inside the same caves under Mahayana influence.

⚠️ Put those together and a development becomes visible that is entirely within this topic's ownership. ⚠️ Early on, the two forms are functionally separate: you live in one and worship in the other. ⚠️ Later, an image is installed in a shrine cut into the residence, and the vihara becomes a place of worship as well as a dwelling. ⚠️ That is the **architectural correlate** of the shift from symbol to image whose art-historical account belongs to the sculpture topic - and stating it as an architectural fact, with Ajanta named, keeps the answer inside its own boundary while still making the point.

### Criticism and reply: does the vihara-chaitya ratio really tell us anything?

⚠️ **The criticism.** Lesson 14 will note that at Ellora only one of the twelve Buddhist caves is a chaitya, and will read from this a monastic emphasis. But excavation is expensive and a chaitya hall is far more expensive than a cell block; a low ratio might reflect cost rather than preference.

⚠️ **The reply, which concedes something.** The objection is good and the correct response is to weaken the claim rather than defend it whole. ⚠️ A ratio of viharas to chaityas is evidence of what a community *built*, which is a joint product of what it wanted and what it could afford; and since one chaitya can serve a large resident population while cells cannot, a single hall with many viharas may indicate a functioning arrangement rather than a de-emphasis of worship. ⚠️ The defensible formulation is therefore: the proportion suggests a community organised principally around residence, with congregational space provided once rather than repeatedly - an inference from proportion, not a documented statement.

### Examination use

**Where it sits.** ✅ The chaitya-vihara pair is definitional vocabulary for every cave site in the syllabus and one of the most frequently tested distinctions in the topic.

**Probable framing.** ⚠️ Compare chaitya and vihara; explain how plan reflects function; any site question where the candidate must say what a numbered cave *is*.

**Traps.**

1. ❌ Reversing the two terms. ✅ Chaitya = worship hall; vihara = residence.
2. ❌ Saying both types developed simultaneously. ✅ The vihara developed during the Mauryan period; the chaitya hall developed in the post-Mauryan period.
3. ❌ Asserting one universal chaitya shape. ⚠️ Lead with function; describe the developed apsidal type; note variation.
4. ⚠️ Forgetting the practical features - open courtyards and stone screen walls to shield from rain.
5. ❌ Importing the sculptural account of the symbol-to-image shift instead of stating its architectural correlate.

### Revision notes

1. ✅ Two post-Mauryan rock-cut types: **chaitya** (prayer/assembly hall) and **vihara** (residence for Buddhist and Jain monks).
2. ✅ **Viharas developed during the Mauryan Empire**; **chaitya halls developed in the post-Mauryan period**.
3. ⚠️ Developed rock-cut chaitya: **apsidal end**, **stupa at the terminal end**, **colonnades** forming aisles, **vaulted roof** - each element serving circumambulation and timber-prototype memory.
4. ✅ The core account's general description of chaitya halls is of **quadrangular chambers with flat roofs** used as prayer halls - a recorded difference from the apsidal description; lead with function and note the variation.
5. ✅ Caves also had **open courtyards** and **stone screen walls** to shield from rain, with human and animal decoration.
6. ✅ A **shrine room introduced into the vihara** is recorded as an innovation of the later Ajanta phase.
7. ⚠️ The colonnade is the pradakshina patha taken indoors; the vault is a timber roof reproduced in rock.
8. ⚠️ The vihara-chaitya ratio is evidence of what a community built, and any inference from it must allow for cost and for one hall serving many residents.

### Concept check

**Question:** Explain why a chaitya hall is apsidal and colonnaded, using the ritual it was built for, and then state the limit of that explanation.

**Model answer:** Both features serve circumambulation. The object of worship is a stupa placed at the terminal end of the hall, and the act of veneration is walking around it, as in the open-air monument where the vedika defines a pradakshina patha at ground level. A rounded, apsidal end allows that circuit to run continuously behind the stupa instead of forcing worshippers around square corners, and the two rows of pillars that form the colonnade divide the hall into a central nave and side aisles, so that the aisles function as the circumambulatory path brought indoors; the vaulted roof reproduces in rock the curved ceiling of a timber-and-thatch hall, the same translation of a wooden prototype that appears on the Lomas Rishi facade. The limit is that this describes the developed rock-cut type rather than every structure called a chaitya: the core account's own general sentence describes chaitya halls as mainly quadrangular chambers with flat roofs used as prayer halls, so plan and roof form vary, and an answer should lead with the function that is constant before describing the form that is not.

**Misconception to avoid:** Learning the two words as a vocabulary pair to be matched, rather than as two solutions to two different needs. A candidate who holds "residence" and "assembly around an object of worship" can classify an unfamiliar cave from its plan; a candidate who holds only the labels will guess.

## Lesson 12 - The western Deccan sequence: trade routes, donors and incomplete evidence

Progress: 12/18 | Stage: Core | Subtopic: the Satavahana-period western Deccan sites of Karle, Bhaja, Nasik, Bedsa, Kondane and Pitalkhora; the second recorded group of Junnar, Kanheri and Kuda described as simple viharas; Nasik in detail as the group of 23 caves with its Hinayana phase, later Mahayana influence, rock-cut water tanks and the Gautamiputra vihara dedicated under Vasishthiputra Pulumavi; what a trade-route distribution implies about donors; and an honest statement of what the incomplete evidence does not allow

━━━ PRE-TEACH CHECKLIST ━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━
Book context: queried - the core account's table of notable post-Mauryan architecture, whose Satavahana row lists Karle, Bhaja, Nasik, Bedsa, Kondane and Pitalkhora in Maharashtra and whose feature description is incomplete in the text available, and whose second row lists Junnar, Kanheri and Kuda as simple viharas under an incomplete patron label; and the Nasik Caves passage, which is complete and supplies the cave count, the names Pandav Leni and Trirashmi Leni, the date range, the Hinayana and later Mahayana phases, the water management and Cave No. 3
CA search: not sought - the single current linkage in this session sits in Lesson 14 and is limited there
CA found: none carried for this lesson
━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━

### Visual: caves on a trade corridor

```text
        WESTERN GHATS / SAHYADRI - the escarpment that the routes cross
        =============================================================
         coastal ports  <----- passes through the Ghats ----->  Deccan
         (Konkan side)                                          plateau
                                                                interior
              \                    |                    /
               \       KANHERI     |     JUNNAR        /
                \        KUDA      |     NASIK        /
                 \                 |   (Pandav Leni)  /
                  \    BHAJA   KARLE   BEDSA         /
                   \   KONDANE    PITALKHORA        /
                    \_______________|______________/
                                    |
                                    v
          CAVES CLUSTER WHERE THE ROUTES RUN, NOT WHERE THE PALACES ARE
                                    |
              +---------------------+---------------------+
              |                                           |
     SATAVAHANA ROW (1st c. BCE - 2nd c. CE)      SECOND ROW
     Karle, Bhaja, Nasik, Bedsa, Kondane,         Junnar, Kanheri, Kuda
     Pitalkhora (Maharashtra)                     = SIMPLE VIHARAS
     Buddhist group; includes both VIHARAS        patron label incomplete
     and CHAITYA halls                            in the available text
              |                                           |
              +---------------------+---------------------+
                                    v
              WHAT MAY BE WRITTEN: the site lists, the recorded patron
              and feature labels, and the complete Nasik account
              WHAT MAY NOT BE WRITTEN: any completion of an incomplete
              label, or a description of Karle or Bhaja built from one
```

⚠️ The lower box is not a disclaimer; it is the lesson's method. Incomplete evidence is a common condition of historical work, and the correct response is to state the boundary of what the evidence supports.

### 1. The geography is the argument

⚠️ Look again at where these caves are. They are cut into the escarpment of the Western Ghats, at and around the passes that connect the western coast to the Deccan interior. ⚠️ They are not at capitals. They are not at battlefields. They are on the roads.

⚠️ That distribution is the single most powerful inference available in this lesson, and it is worth stating carefully because it is an inference. ⚠️ Excavating and endowing a cave complex requires surplus wealth. Surplus wealth on a trade corridor is generated by trade. Religious establishments on such a corridor also perform a service for traders - shelter, water, a stopping place, and a community to whom gifts can be made for merit. ⚠️ The reasonable conclusion is that this cave landscape reflects a *mercantile* donor world operating under Satavahana political authority, rather than a purely royal building programme.

⚠️ Two independent facts from elsewhere in this session support the inference without proving it. ✅ The Sanchi lintel inscription records a gift by artisans, not by a king. ✅ The Amaravati inscriptions record patronage patterns and socio-religious life. ⚠️ Both show that the recorded donors at Buddhist establishments of this period were frequently non-royal, which is exactly what the road-side distribution would predict.

### 2. What the source lists, and where it stops

✅ The account's table of notable post-Mauryan architecture places under the **Satavahanas (1st century BCE - 2nd century CE)** the major sites **Karle, Bhaja, Nasik, Bedsa, Kondane** and **Pitalkhora**, in **Maharashtra**. ✅ A second row lists **Junnar, Kanheri** and **Kuda** with the feature description **"Simple Viharas"**.

⚠️ **A limitation to state plainly.** Beyond the site lists, the description of this group is incomplete in the text available for this session, and the patron label of the second row is incomplete too. ❌ No incomplete label is completed here, no site is described from one, and no patron dynasty is supplied for the second row. ⚠️ What the recorded material does support is that the group is Buddhist and that it includes both viharas and chaitya halls, with a horse-shoe form and a wooden association among its recorded features.

⚠️ This is why Nasik carries the weight of the lesson: its account is complete, and a complete case is worth more in an answer than six half-known names.

### 3. Nasik, in full

✅ The **Nasik Caves** are a group of **23 Buddhist caves**, also known as **"Pandav Leni"** or **"Trirashmi Leni"**. ✅ They were **perhaps carved between the 1st century BC and the 3rd century AD** and belong to the **Hinayana** period. ✅ Later, the influence of the **Mahayana** sect can also be found in them. ✅ Under the Hinayana sect, the Buddha's presence is indicated through the use of **motifs and symbols like a throne and footprints**; later, **idols of the Buddha** were also carved inside these caves, representing Mahayana influence. ✅ The site also displays an **excellent system of water management**, indicated by **water tanks carved out of solid rock**. ✅ **Cave No. 3** is the most important cave, known as the **Gautamiputra vihara (c. 150 CE)**; it was **dedicated to the Buddhist Sangha during the reign of Vasishthiputra Pulumavi**, a Satavahana king.

⚠️ Three points to extract. ⚠️ First, the water tanks are not a curiosity: a cave complex on a hot escarpment housing a resident community must solve water storage before anything else, and cutting tanks from solid rock is a capital investment in habitability. ⚠️ Second, the Hinayana-to-Mahayana sequence is visible **inside the same caves**, in the change from symbol to carved image - a chronology readable from the walls. ⚠️ Third, Cave 3 gives a dated, named dedication under a named Satavahana ruler, which is exactly the sort of anchor an answer needs when it claims Satavahana-period patronage in the western Deccan.

⚠️ Note also the identification discipline: Nasik's caves are **hill caves**, and they are identified by their own names - **Pandav Leni** and **Trirashmi Leni** - and by the group of twenty-three Buddhist excavations, which is the identifier to carry into an objective item.

### 4. What this sequence contributes to the topic

⚠️ Place the western Deccan between Barabar and Ajanta and the sequence becomes an argument about scale and institutionalisation.

| Phase | ✅ What exists | ⚠️ What it implies |
|---|---|---|
| Mauryan, Barabar | A handful of polished granite chambers, endowed by the ruling house for an ascetic sect | Excavation is a royal act, and the beneficiaries are small ascetic communities |
| Western Deccan, Satavahana period | Multiple complexes along the Ghats routes, with viharas, chaitya halls, water systems, and a named dedication to the Sangha | Excavation has become a repeatable institutional practice supported by a broad donor world on trade corridors |
| Ajanta and Ellora | Very large complexes with shrine rooms, painting programmes and, at Ellora, an entire temple carved from one rock | Excavation supports permanent, richly endowed establishments, and by the last phase the technique is applied to non-Buddhist monumental building |

⚠️ An answer that moves along that table has shown *development* rather than listing sites, and it has kept the claim modest: what changes is scale, institutional depth and the range of patrons, not a sudden aesthetic revolution.

### Examination use

**Where it sits.** ✅ This lesson supplies the middle term of any chronological account of Indian rock-cut architecture and the evidence for the donor-society argument.

**Probable framing.** ⚠️ The evolution of rock-cut architecture; patronage of Buddhist establishments; site-identification items on Nasik; and any question about what cave distribution reveals about economy and society.

**Traps.**

1. ⚠️ Nasik is identified as a hill-cave group by its own names, Pandav Leni and Trirashmi Leni, and by its twenty-three Buddhist excavations.
2. ❌ Completing an incomplete label or describing Karle and Bhaja from one.
3. ❌ Assigning Junnar, Kanheri and Kuda to a named dynasty; the patron label is incomplete and no dynasty is supplied here.
4. ⚠️ Treating the trade-route inference as documented fact. ⚠️ It is an inference from distribution, supported by donor inscriptions elsewhere.
5. ❌ Confusing Nasik's Cave 3 dedication: it is the **Gautamiputra vihara**, dedicated to the Sangha during the reign of **Vasishthiputra Pulumavi**.

### Revision notes

1. ✅ Satavahana-row sites (1st century BCE - 2nd century CE), Maharashtra: **Karle, Bhaja, Nasik, Bedsa, Kondane, Pitalkhora**.
2. ✅ Second row: **Junnar, Kanheri, Kuda** - "**Simple Viharas**"; the patron label is incomplete in the text available and is not completed here.
3. ✅ **Nasik**: **23** Buddhist caves; **Pandav Leni / Trirashmi Leni**; perhaps **1st century BC to 3rd century AD**; **Hinayana** period, with later **Mahayana** influence.
4. ✅ Hinayana phase: Buddha indicated by **motifs and symbols - throne and footprints**; later **idols** carved in the same caves.
5. ✅ **Water tanks carved out of solid rock** - an excellent system of water management.
6. ✅ **Cave No. 3 = Gautamiputra vihara, c. 150 CE**, dedicated to the Buddhist **Sangha** during the reign of **Vasishthiputra Pulumavi**.
7. ⚠️ Distribution argument: caves cluster on the Ghats trade corridors, implying a mercantile donor world under Satavahana authority - an inference supported by non-royal donor inscriptions at Sanchi and Amaravati.
8. ⚠️ Development across the topic: royal ascetic endowment (Barabar) → repeatable institutional practice on trade routes (western Deccan) → very large endowed establishments and monolithic carving (Ajanta, Ellora).
9. ✅ Nasik is a hill-cave group, identified by its own names rather than by a river feature.
10. ⚠️ Limitation: the description of the notable post-Mauryan architecture group is incomplete in the text available; only what is recorded is used.

### Concept check

**Question:** What does the *location* of the western Deccan caves allow you to argue about who paid for them, and what would you need in order to prove it?

**Model answer:** The location allows an argument from distribution. The Satavahana-period sites named in the source - Karle, Bhaja, Nasik, Bedsa, Kondane and Pitalkhora, with Junnar, Kanheri and Kuda recorded as simple viharas - lie along the Western Ghats escarpment, at and around the passes connecting the western coast to the Deccan interior, rather than at political capitals. Since excavation requires surplus wealth, and surplus wealth on such a corridor is generated by trade, and since religious establishments on a route provide shelter, stored water and a community to whom merit-earning gifts can be made, the reasonable conclusion is that a mercantile donor world sustained these complexes under Satavahana political authority. Proving it requires donor inscriptions naming the givers at these sites, and what the material held here supplies instead is indirect corroboration from comparable sites: the Siri-Satakarni inscription at Sanchi records a gift by the artisans of a king rather than by the king, and the Amaravati inscriptions are recorded as describing patronage patterns and socio-religious life. The claim should therefore be stated as a well-supported inference, with the named Nasik dedication of Cave 3 to the Sangha in the reign of Vasishthiputra Pulumavi cited as the secure royal-period anchor.

**Misconception to avoid:** Assuming that a Satavahana-period date means Satavahana royal funding. Period labels describe political context; they do not identify donors. The distinction is the same one that governs the Sanchi gateways, and it is tested more often than any stylistic point in this lesson.

---

## Lesson 13 - Ajanta: two phases, five chaityas and a gorge that identifies the site

### Visual: the horseshoe, and the two phases inside it

```text
        THE WAGHORA RIVER GORGE, SAHYADRI HILLS, AURANGABAD DISTRICT
        ============================================================
                    the river cuts a HORSE-SHOE bend;
                 the caves are arranged along that curve

              ___________________________________
            /                                     \
           /   [9] [10]            [19] [26] [29]   \     <- the FIVE CHAITYAS
          |     chaityas             chaityas        |       (9, 10, 19, 26, 29)
          |                                          |
          |  [1] [2] [4] [6] [11] [16] [17] ... etc  |    <- the REST are VIHARAS
           \                                        /
            \______________________________________/
                         WAGHORA river below

   PHASE I - SATAVAHANA                PHASE II - VAKATAKA
   c. 2nd c. BC - 1st c. AD            c. 5th - 6th c. AD
   5 caves                             24 caves
   HINAYANA (Theravada) phase          MAHAYANA phase
   object of worship = a STUPA         Buddha image; SHRINE ROOM
                                       introduced into the VIHARA
                                       Cave 1 patronised by Vakataka
                                       king HARISHENA (c. 460-477 CE)
                                       Vakatakas = contemporaries of
                                       the Imperial Guptas

   TECHNIQUE: mural paintings - done on ALREADY DRIED surfaces
              FRESCO SECCO, SIX colours: white, red, yellow,
              black, green, blue
```

⚠️ The identification half of this diagram is worth as much in an examination as the art-historical half. Ajanta is the **only** site in this session's evidence that carries a river-gorge identifier, which is why the gorge, rather than the district, is the fact to hold.

### 1. The identification set, stated first because it is most tested

✅ The spectacular rock-cut Buddhist site of Ajanta **lies in the gorge of the Waghora river**, in the **Sahyadri hills**, in **Aurangabad district of Maharashtra**.

⚠️ Three identifiers, of unequal strength. ⚠️ "Aurangabad district" is the weakest, because other sites share the district. ⚠️ "Sahyadri hills" is regional and shared with Ellora. ⚠️ **The Waghora gorge is unique to Ajanta in this corpus**, and it is therefore the discriminator to hold.

⚠️ The method generalises: a site-identification question is answered by pairing a site with the **one** feature that belongs to it alone - the Waghora gorge for Ajanta, the Baghni bank for Bagh, the island for Elephanta, the twin hills near Bhubaneswar for Udayagiri-Khandagiri, the granite hills of Jehanabad for Barabar, the Krishna river and the ancient names for Amaravati. ⚠️ Where a site's identifier is of another kind - a hilltop complex at Sanchi, a hill-cave group at Nasik - that other feature is what the identification rests on.

✅ A British lieutenant, **John Smith**, came upon the caves in **1819** during a hunt.

### 2. Two phases, and why the division matters more than the total

✅ There are **29 Buddhist caves** at Ajanta: **5** belong to the **Satavahana period** (c. 2nd century BC to 1st century AD) and **24** belong to the **Vakataka period** (c. 5th to 6th century AD). ✅ All the caves are arranged in a **horse-shoe shape**. ✅ Only **5** of them - **caves 9, 10, 19, 26 and 29** - were **chaityas**, places of worship; the rest were **viharas**, dwelling places for monks. ✅ **Cave no. 1** is the most sumptuous rock-cut vihara ever made in India, patronised by the Vakataka king **Harishena**.

✅ The earliest excavations were done by the **Satavahanas** and belong to the **Hinayana (Theravada)** phase of Buddhism, where the **object of worship is a stupa**. ✅ Phase II excavations are a major contribution of the **Vakataka** dynasty, particularly under **Harishena (c. 460-477 CE)**, and belong to the **Mahayana** phase; the **Vakatakas were contemporaries of the Imperial Guptas**.

⚠️ **A caution the evidence requires.** Ajanta's numbered cave total varies with whether an unfinished or late-identified excavation is counted, and the count should never be made the analytical point of an answer. ⚠️ The recorded figure is twenty-nine, and this session states twenty-nine as the recorded figure - while making the argument out of the **division**, not the total. ⚠️ The division is what carries meaning: a long interruption between two campaigns separated by roughly five centuries, with a change of Buddhist orientation across the gap.

⚠️ That gap is the site's most examinable feature. ⚠️ Ajanta is not a continuously developing site; it is two campaigns at the same place, and the second campaign's architecture records a doctrinal change - the stupa-centred hall giving way to caves in which an image occupies a shrine.

### 3. The caves that carry the argument

✅ Some caves have **two storeys**, showing advanced engineering and planning - **Cave 6** is the example given. ✅ **Cave 26** enshrines a huge stupa with a **seated Buddha carved in high relief**; on its left wall the Buddha is shown in a reclining pose representing the **parinibbana**, the final nirvana, surrounded by figures in mourning. ✅ Like the chaityas, the viharas display a profusion of sculptural ornamentation, and the **introduction of a shrine room into the vihara is an innovation of this period**.

⚠️ Cave 26 is worth a sentence of analysis because it shows the transition inside one chamber: a stupa - the older object of worship - now carries a Buddha image carved into it in high relief. ⚠️ The architectural form is inherited; the focus of devotion has changed. ⚠️ That is the clearest single illustration of what the second phase did, and it is an architectural observation, which keeps it inside this topic's boundary.

### 4. The paintings, described with the correct technical care

✅ Originally, **most of the caves had mural paintings** - paintings done on **already dried surfaces** - but a few survive today. ✅ The **fresco secco** technique is used, with **six colours**: white, red, yellow, black, green and blue. ✅ The murals depict scenes connected with the **Buddha, Bodhisattvas and Jatakas**, along with **Yakshas, Gandharvas and Apsaras**. ✅ The most notable scenes are from the **Vessantara Jataka** and of **Bodhisattva Padmapani** - the compassionate Buddha holding a lotus - and **Vajrapani**. ✅ Other notable paintings include **The Dying Princess** (Cave 16), **The King Janaka and his Queen** (Cave 1) and **The Temptation of Mara** (Cave 26). ✅ In addition to religious scenes there are many scenes of everyday life, animals and nature.

⚠️ Two precision points. ⚠️ First, the technical definition: painting on an **already dried** surface is what *secco* means, as against painting into wet plaster. Stating the definition rather than only the term is what distinguishes an informed answer. ⚠️ Second, the **everyday-life** content matters evidentially: a religious monument whose paintings include ordinary life, animals and nature is a source for social history and not only for doctrine - a point that feeds directly into the rock-cut-as-source argument of Lesson 18.

⚠️ The detailed art-historical analysis of the painting tradition - style, composition, comparison with other schools - belongs to the painting topic. ⚠️ This session records technique, subjects and named works because they are architectural fittings of the caves it owns, and it stops there.

### 5. External testimony and current status

✅ References to the Ajanta caves are found in the travel accounts of the Chinese Buddhist travellers **Fa Hien**, who came during the reign of Chandragupta II in the 4th-5th century, and **Hieun Tsang**, who came in the 7th century. ✅ The caves were designated a **UNESCO World Heritage Site in 1983**.

⚠️ Use the travellers as corroboration of a site's active life, not as descriptions of its architecture - the same discipline applied to Megasthenes in Lesson 2.

### Comparative note: Ajanta against Nasik, and what the pairing shows

⚠️ Both sites are in Maharashtra, both begin in a Hinayana phase, and both later show Mahayana influence. ⚠️ The comparison is useful because it separates **scale** from **sequence**. ✅ At Nasik, the sequence is visible as a change of practice inside a modest group of twenty-three caves - symbols giving way to carved images. ✅ At Ajanta, the same sequence is visible as two distinct building campaigns separated by centuries, the second of them enormously better endowed and producing the most sumptuous rock-cut vihara in India under a named king. ⚠️ The doctrinal change is therefore not a function of wealth, while the *scale* at which it is expressed clearly is - and separating those two variables is precisely the kind of move that lifts a comparative answer.

### Examination use

**Where it sits.** ✅ Ajanta is the standard named site for rock-cut architecture, for painting and for phased patronage, and it is the topic's only gorge-identified site.

**Probable framing.** ⚠️ Site-identification items pairing a site with a river or landform; any question on cave architecture, mural painting or Vakataka patronage; the rock-cut-as-source demand.

**Traps.**

1. ❌ Attaching the Waghora gorge to another site, or giving Sanchi, Nasik or Amaravati a gorge.
2. ⚠️ Making the cave total the analytical point. ✅ State the source's figure of 29 and argue from the 5-and-24 division.
3. ❌ Reversing the phases. ✅ Satavahana-period Hinayana first, Vakataka-period Mahayana second.
4. ❌ Calling the murals frescoes painted on wet plaster. ✅ They are done on already dried surfaces, in fresco secco, with six colours.
5. ❌ Misplacing the named paintings. ✅ The Dying Princess in Cave 16; King Janaka and his Queen in Cave 1; The Temptation of Mara in Cave 26.
6. ⚠️ Giving Ajanta the wrong inscription year. ✅ 1983, the same year as Ellora.

### Revision notes

1. ✅ Location: **gorge of the Waghora river**, **Sahyadri hills**, **Aurangabad district, Maharashtra** - the gorge is the unique discriminator.
2. ✅ **John Smith**, a British lieutenant, came upon the caves in **1819** during a hunt.
3. ✅ **29 caves**: **5** Satavahana-period (c. 2nd century BC - 1st century AD), **24** Vakataka-period (c. 5th-6th century AD); all arranged in a **horse-shoe** shape.
4. ✅ **Five chaityas: caves 9, 10, 19, 26, 29**; the rest are **viharas**.
5. ✅ Phase I = **Hinayana (Theravada)**, object of worship a **stupa**. Phase II = **Mahayana**, chiefly under **Harishena (c. 460-477 CE)**; the **Vakatakas were contemporaries of the Imperial Guptas**.
6. ✅ **Cave 1** = the most sumptuous rock-cut vihara in India, patronised by **Harishena**; **Cave 6** is two-storeyed; **Cave 26** has a huge stupa with a seated Buddha in high relief and a reclining **parinibbana** scene with mourners.
7. ✅ **Shrine room introduced into the vihara** - an innovation of this period.
8. ✅ Murals on **already dried surfaces**, **fresco secco**, **six colours** - white, red, yellow, black, green, blue; subjects include Buddha, Bodhisattvas, Jatakas, Yakshas, Gandharvas, Apsaras, and everyday life, animals and nature.
9. ✅ Named works: **Vessantara Jataka**; **Bodhisattva Padmapani** (lotus) and **Vajrapani**; **The Dying Princess** (Cave 16); **The King Janaka and his Queen** (Cave 1); **The Temptation of Mara** (Cave 26).
10. ✅ **Fa Hien** (reign of Chandragupta II, 4th-5th century) and **Hieun Tsang** (7th century) refer to the caves. ✅ **UNESCO World Heritage Site, 1983**.
11. ⚠️ Argue from the two-campaign division, not from the total; the count varies with what is counted.

### Concept check

**Question:** Ajanta is often described as a single great achievement of Indian art. Why is "two campaigns" a more accurate description, and what does the second campaign's architecture record that the first does not?

**Model answer:** Because the caves were excavated in two widely separated periods rather than continuously. The source records twenty-nine Buddhist caves, of which five belong to the Satavahana period, roughly the second century BC to the first century AD, and twenty-four to the Vakataka period, roughly the fifth to sixth centuries AD - a gap of several centuries between the campaigns. The first campaign belongs to the Hinayana or Theravada phase, in which the object of worship is a stupa, so the chaityas of that phase are halls built around a stupa. The second, largely under Harishena between about 460 and 477 CE, belongs to the Mahayana phase, and its architecture records a change of devotional focus: a shrine room is introduced into the vihara, which had previously been purely residential, and in Cave 26 a huge stupa carries a seated Buddha carved in high relief with a reclining parinibbana scene on the left wall. So the second campaign preserves the inherited form while changing what stands at its centre, and it does so at a far greater scale - Cave 1, patronised by Harishena, is described as the most sumptuous rock-cut vihara ever made in India.

**Misconception to avoid:** Fixing on the cave total. The number varies according to whether unfinished or later-identified excavations are counted, and an answer built on the total is fragile; an answer built on the division between the two campaigns is both secure and analytically useful.

## Lesson 14 - Ellora: thirty-four caves, three religions, one monolith

Progress: 14/18 | Stage: Core | Subtopic: location in the Sahyadri range about a hundred kilometres from Ajanta; the thirty-four caves and their division into twelve Buddhist, seventeen Hindu and five Jain; overlapping rather than clean sequential phases; Cave 10 as the only Buddhist chaitya and its carpentry finish; Cave 12 Tin Thal; Cave 16, the monolithic Kailasha of Krishna I; Cave 15 Dashavatara and the Dantidurga inscription; Caves 21 and 29; the Jain caves and their names; the Baroda copper-plate naming Elapura; the vihara-predominance inference; the 1983 inscription; and the single dated current linkage of this session

Book context: queried - the core account's Ellora Caves passage, which records the location and distance from Ajanta, the Baroda inscription of 812 AD naming Elapura and the Kailasa temple built by Krishnaraja, the 5th-11th century range, the 34 caves and their religious division, the single Buddhist chaitya, the images and mudras, Cave 10 Vishvakarma and its wooden-beam finish, Cave 12 Tin Thal, Cave 16 Kailasha under Krishna I, Cave 15 Dashavatara, Cave 21 Rameshwar Lena, Cave 29 Dhumar Lena, the named Jain caves, the Dantidurga inscription and the 1983 inscription
CA search: "Ellora Caves official conservation and state-of-conservation statement, UNESCO World Heritage Centre, consulted 29 September 2026"
CA found: UNESCO World Heritage Centre property page for the Ellora Caves, consulted 29 September 2026 - carries the official statement of outstanding universal value, a three-phase excavation chronology, the identified threats to integrity and the statement that management is carried out by the Archaeological Survey of India under the Ancient Monuments and Archaeological Sites and Remains Act, 1958
━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━

### Visual: one hillside, three traditions, and the cave that is a temple

```text
   ELLORA - Sahyadri range, Maharashtra, about 100 km from AJANTA
   ==============================================================
   34 CAVES, numbered continuously along the escarpment

   caves 1-12      BUDDHIST    Mahayana and Vajrayana traditions
                               * only ONE is a prayer hall (CHAITYA)
                               * the other eleven are MONASTERIES (viharas)
                               * Cave 10 = VISHVAKARMA / "Carpenter's Cave":
                                 the rock is finished to LOOK LIKE WOODEN
                                 BEAMS; a massive seated Buddha in
                                 vyakhyana mudra in front of a votive stupa
                               * Cave 12 = TIN THAL, three storeys; panels
                                 of SEVEN Manushi Buddhas and SEVEN
                                 Dhyani Buddhas
   ---------------------------------------------------------------
   caves 13-29     HINDU       * Cave 16 = KAILASHA: a MONOLITHIC temple
                                 carved from a single rock, dedicated to
                                 Shiva, built by KRISHNA I of the
                                 RASHTRAKUTA dynasty, 8th century
                               * Cave 15 = DASHAVATARA; carries the
                                 DANTIDURGA inscription (8th century)
                               * Cave 21 = RAMESHWAR LENA (Kalachuri)
                               * Cave 29 = DHUMAR LENA
   ---------------------------------------------------------------
   caves 30-34     JAIN        DIGAMBARA sect
                               * Caves 30, 32, 33 = CHHOTA KAILASH,
                                 INDRA SABHA, JAGANNATH SABHA
   ===============================================================
   CHRONOLOGY WARNING: the three groups BROADLY sequence but the
   phases OVERLAP. ❌ Do not present a clean Buddhist-then-Hindu-
   then-Jain relay.

   THE MONOLITH, IN ONE LINE:
   ordinary temple = stones ASSEMBLED upward from the ground
   Kailasha        = a hillside cut DOWNWARD until a temple is left
```

⚠️ The last two lines of the diagram are the reason Ellora closes the rock-cut story. Every earlier site excavates *interior space*. Kailasha excavates *a building*.

### 1. The site

✅ The Ellora Caves are located around **100 km from the Ajanta caves** and form part of the **Sahyadri range** hills in **Maharashtra**. ✅ The rock-cut caves were built roughly between the **5th and 11th centuries AD**; like Ajanta they are prominent examples of rich architecture and mural painting, though they are much newer than the Ajanta caves. ✅ The short form of the old name **Elloorpuram** is Ellora, sometimes known as Verul or Elura, and the older form of the word has been found in historical references such as the **Baroda inscription of 812 AD**.

✅ Of the total **34 caves**, there are **12 Buddhist caves** of the **Mahayana and Vajrayana** tradition (caves 1-12), **17 Hindu caves** (caves 13-29), and **5 Jain caves** belonging to the **Digambara** sect (caves 30-34). ✅ On that basis the account states that Ellora is one of the most spectacular examples of religious coexistence.

⚠️ Hold the coexistence claim at the strength the evidence supports. ⚠️ What is demonstrated is that one hillside was found suitable and sacred by three traditions across several centuries, and that each was endowed well enough to cut permanent architecture there. ❌ What is not demonstrated is a planned inter-faith project or a single patron's tolerance programme, and ❌ the phases are not a clean, non-overlapping relay.

### 2. The Buddhist group, and the carpentry that will not go away

✅ Caves 1-12 are Buddhist caves built by the **Vakatakas and the Chalukya dynasty**. ✅ **Only one of the twelve Buddhist caves is a prayer hall, that is, a chaitya**; the rest are monasteries, or viharas. ✅ The Buddhist caves hold images including **Tara, Mahamayuri, Akshobya, Avalokiteshwara, Maitreya and Amitabha**, and the central figure is the Buddha in three sacred postures - meditating (**dhyana mudra**), preaching (**vyakhyana mudra**) and touching the earth with the index finger of the right hand (**bhumi-sparsha mudra**).

✅ The notable Buddhist cave is **Cave 10**, a chaitya known as the **Vishvakarma cave**, also called the **Carpenter's Cave**, because the rock has been given a finish that has the appearance of **wooden beams**; it features a massive **seated Buddha in vyakhyana mudra, carved in front of a votive stupa**. ✅ **Cave 12**, known as **Tin Thal**, consists of **three storeys** and displays large-scale sculptures, with one panel depicting a row of **seven Manushi Buddhas** and a second showing **seven Dhyani Buddhas**.

⚠️ Cave 10 closes a circle opened in Lesson 6. ⚠️ The first Indian excavators at Lomas Rishi carved a facade that reproduced a thatched hut; centuries later, at Ellora, a ceiling is finished to imitate timber beams. ⚠️ The translation of carpentry into rock is not an early experiment that was outgrown - it is a persistent habit of the tradition, and naming both examples in an answer demonstrates continuity across nearly a millennium with two concrete cases.

⚠️ The vihara-predominance point belongs here. ⚠️ Eleven monasteries to one prayer hall suggests a community organised principally around residence, with congregational space provided once rather than repeatedly - an inference from proportion, not a documented statement, and subject to the cost qualification argued in Lesson 11.

### 3. Kailasha: the cave that is a temple

✅ **Cave 16**, containing the **Kailasha temple**, is of great Hindu significance. ✅ It is a **monolithic temple structure, carved out of a single solid rock**, dedicated to **Shiva**, **built by Krishna I of the Rashtrakuta dynasty in the 8th century**, and believed to be the **largest monolith Hindu temple in the world**. ✅ The two-storeyed temple houses a **shivalingam**, with a **flat-roofed mandapa supported by 16 pillars**, along with a **Dravidian shikhara**. ✅ The exterior has images of Shaivite and Vaishnavite deities, and the courtyard has **two huge pillars with the flagstaff** and a **Nandi mandapa**. ✅ Notable sculptures in Cave 16 include **Ravana shaking Mount Kailasha**, the **wedding ceremony of Shiva and Parvati** and the **destruction of Mahishasura by Durga**, with further depictions of Shiva as **Nataraja**, as **Lingodbhava** - the emergence of Shiva as an endless pillar of light - **Andhakasuravadha** and the **Kalyanasundara**; among Vaishnavite sculptures, the avatars of Vishnu are depicted. ✅ One striking feature of the Ellora caves is the **Atiriktanga-Bhairava** sculpture, a skeletal form of Shiva.

✅ **Cave 15** contains a further marvel named the **Dashavatara temple**. ✅ **Cave 21**, also known as **Rameshwar Lena**, is an early excavation by the **Kalachuri** dynasty, with large sculptures of the goddesses **Ganga and Yamuna** flanking the entrance. ✅ **Cave 29**, also known as **Dhumar Lena**, is one of Ellora's earliest and greatest excavations. ✅ Several inscriptions at Ellora date from the **6th century** onwards, the best known being an inscription of the **Rashtrakuta king Dantidurga**, of the **8th century**, on the back wall of the front mandapa of **Cave 15**, stating that he had offered prayers at that temple.

⚠️ **Why Kailasha changes the argument.** Ordinary architecture is additive: stones are assembled upward, and an error can be corrected by replacing a member. Kailasha is subtractive at the scale of an entire temple: the hillside was cut away downward and inward until a complete two-storeyed temple with courtyard, mandapa, shikhara and free-standing pillars remained. ⚠️ There is no possibility of correction, which means the whole building had to be planned in three dimensions before the first cut. ⚠️ That is a statement about *organisation and design capability*, not about beauty, and it is the strongest such statement in the topic.

### 4. The Jain group, and the textual corroboration

✅ **Caves 30, 32 and 33** are Jain caves and consist of **Chhota Kailash, Indra Sabha** and **Jagannath Sabha** respectively, and were commissioned by the **Yadava** dynasty.

✅ The **Baroda inscription** mentions **Elapura**, or Ellora, by referencing the **Kailasa temple built by Krishnaraja**. ✅ This **copper-plate grant by Karkaraja II** records Krishnaraja's patronage of the Kailasanatha temple at Elapura and describes the temple as **a wonder that astonished even celestial beings and the architect**, highlighting its magnificence and establishing the site's antiquity.

⚠️ This is a rare and valuable evidential situation: a text that corroborates a surviving building rather than merely commemorating a ruler. ⚠️ The qualification is that it is a **eulogistic grant**, so its rhetoric - astonishment, celestial beings - is praise rather than description. ⚠️ An answer that cites it as independent textual witness to the monument's construction and patronage, while noting that its language is eulogy, has used it correctly.

⚠️ Set the Baroda grant against the Hathigumpha inscription of the next lesson and the pair becomes a complete lesson in epigraphy: one inscription **corroborates** a monument, the other **omits** an event. ⚠️ Together they show that inscriptions are evidence of what a patron wished recorded.

### 5. Current status, and this session's single dated linkage

✅ The Ellora Cave Complex was designated a **UNESCO World Heritage Site in 1983**.

📰 **Current linkage - used once in this session and limited here.** The UNESCO World Heritage Centre's property page for the Ellora Caves, consulted on **29 September 2026**, records the official statement of outstanding universal value for the property: an ensemble of 34 caves in the Charanandri hills, with rock-cut activity carried out in three phases from the 6th to the 12th century - the earliest caves 1-12 excavated between the 5th and 8th centuries reflecting Mahayana philosophy, the Brahmanical group of caves 13-29 including Cave 16 excavated between the 7th and 10th centuries, and the Jaina group of caves 30-34 excavated between the 9th and 12th centuries. 📰 The same page records that management of the property is carried out by the Archaeological Survey of India, with buffer zones jointly managed by the ASI, the Forest Department and the Government of Maharashtra under legislation including the Ancient Monuments and Archaeological Sites and Remains Act, 1958, and it identifies visitor and environmental management, seepage and cracking in the caves and conservation staffing capacity as potential threats to the property's integrity.

⚠️ **A discrepancy to record rather than resolve.** The core account read for this session states that the Ellora caves were built roughly between the **5th and 11th centuries AD**, while the official property page gives a three-phase chronology running from the **6th to the 12th century**, with the Jain group placed in the 9th-12th centuries. ⚠️ The two are close but not identical, and this session states both rather than choosing the convenient one. ⚠️ In an answer, the safe formulation is that excavation at Ellora ran across several centuries in three broad religious phases that overlapped, with the Kailasha temple securely placed in the 8th century under Krishna I.

⚠️ Everything beyond this paragraph - conservation programming, monument statistics, management policy - is handed to the heritage-institutions topic, and no further current item appears anywhere in this session.

### Examination use

**Where it sits.** ✅ Ellora is the standard named site for multi-religious cave landscapes, for monolithic carving and for the qualified coexistence argument.

**Probable framing.** ⚠️ Religious coexistence in Indian architecture; rock-cut architecture as a source; Rashtrakuta patronage; a statement item on cave numbering and religious attribution.

**Traps.**

1. ❌ Calling Ellora a Buddhist-only site, or forgetting the Jain group.
2. ❌ Presenting a clean Buddhist-then-Hindu-then-Jain sequence. ✅ The phases broadly sequence but overlap.
3. ❌ Converting material plurality into proof of an abstract, uninterrupted harmony.
4. ❌ Confusing the numbering. ✅ 1-12 Buddhist; 13-29 Hindu; 30-34 Jain.
5. ❌ Treating the Baroda grant as neutral description. ⚠️ It is a eulogistic copper-plate grant.
6. ⚠️ Misplacing Kailasha. ✅ Cave 16, monolithic, Shiva, Krishna I, Rashtrakuta, 8th century.

### Revision notes

1. ✅ Ellora: **Sahyadri range, Maharashtra**, about **100 km from Ajanta**; caves built roughly between the **5th and 11th centuries AD**; old name **Elloorpuram**, also Verul or Elura.
2. ✅ **34 caves**: **12 Buddhist** (1-12, Mahayana and Vajrayana), **17 Hindu** (13-29), **5 Jain** (30-34, **Digambara**).
3. ✅ Buddhist caves built by the **Vakatakas and the Chalukya dynasty**; **only one of the twelve is a chaitya**; images include Tara, Mahamayuri, Akshobya, Avalokiteshwara, Maitreya, Amitabha; Buddha in **dhyana**, **vyakhyana** and **bhumi-sparsha** mudras.
4. ✅ **Cave 10 = Vishvakarma / Carpenter's Cave**, a chaitya, rock finished to look like **wooden beams**, with a massive seated Buddha in **vyakhyana mudra** in front of a votive stupa.
5. ✅ **Cave 12 = Tin Thal**, three storeys; panels of **seven Manushi Buddhas** and **seven Dhyani Buddhas**.
6. ✅ **Cave 16 = Kailasha**: **monolithic**, carved from a single rock, dedicated to **Shiva**, by **Krishna I, Rashtrakuta, 8th century**; believed the **largest monolith Hindu temple in the world**; two storeys, shivalingam, flat-roofed **mandapa on 16 pillars**, **Dravidian shikhara**, two flagstaff pillars and a **Nandi mandapa**.
7. ✅ Cave 16 sculptures: **Ravana shaking Mount Kailasha**, the **Shiva-Parvati wedding**, **Durga destroying Mahishasura**, **Nataraja**, **Lingodbhava**, **Andhakasuravadha**, **Kalyanasundara**; Vaishnavite avatars also depicted. ✅ **Atiriktanga-Bhairava**, a skeletal Shiva, is a striking Ellora feature.
8. ✅ **Cave 15 = Dashavatara**, carrying the **Dantidurga** inscription (Rashtrakuta, 8th century) recording that he offered prayers there; **Cave 21 = Rameshwar Lena** (Kalachuri), with **Ganga and Yamuna** at the entrance; **Cave 29 = Dhumar Lena**, among the earliest and greatest.
9. ✅ Jain caves **30, 32, 33 = Chhota Kailash, Indra Sabha, Jagannath Sabha**, commissioned by the **Yadava** dynasty. ✅ Inscriptions at Ellora date from the **6th century** onwards.
10. ✅ **Baroda inscription, 812 AD**, a **copper-plate grant of Karkaraja II**, names **Elapura** and records **Krishnaraja's** patronage of the Kailasanatha temple, describing it as a wonder that astonished even celestial beings and the architect - corroboration, but eulogistic.
11. ✅ **UNESCO World Heritage Site, 1983.** 📰 The property page consulted on 29 September 2026 records a three-phase chronology from the 6th to the 12th century, ASI management under the 1958 Act, and integrity threats including seepage, cracking and visitor management.
12. ⚠️ Kailasha's significance is organisational: a subtractive building admitting no correction had to be fully planned before the first cut.

### Concept check

**Question:** State the strongest defensible claim that Ellora supports about religious coexistence, the claim it does not support, and the evidence that decides between them.

**Model answer:** The defensible claim is that one hillside in the Sahyadri range was found sacred and worth endowing by three traditions over several centuries: of the thirty-four caves, twelve are Buddhist of the Mahayana and Vajrayana traditions, seventeen are Hindu and five are Jain of the Digambara sect, and excavation at the site ran across a long span, with inscriptions dating from the sixth century onwards. The claim it does not support is that this represents a planned inter-faith project or a single patron's tolerance programme, or that the groups succeeded one another in a clean, non-overlapping relay. The deciding evidence is the pattern of patronage and dating: the Buddhist caves are attributed to the Vakatakas and the Chalukya dynasty, Cave 16 to Krishna I of the Rashtrakutas in the eighth century, Cave 21 to the Kalachuris and the Jain caves to the Yadavas, and the Dantidurga inscription of the eighth century records a Rashtrakuta ruler offering prayers at Cave 15 - that is, different dynasties at different times, each endowing its own tradition at a site already in use. The official property page consulted for this session likewise sets out three phases that run across overlapping centuries rather than in sequence. Competitive and successive patronage of a continuously sacred place is the claim the evidence carries.

**Misconception to avoid:** Treating the presence of three religions at one site as a statement about belief. It is a statement about the site and about patronage practice. The sentence that earns marks names the dynasties and the dates; the sentence that loses them substitutes an adjective for them.

## Lesson 15 - Udayagiri and Khandagiri: a Jain royal complex and an inscription that omits

Progress: 15/18 | Stage: Core | Subtopic: the twin hills near Bhubaneswar and the sandstone cave group; Kharavela of the Maha-Meghavahana dynasty and the continuation to the Somavamsis; Udayagiri's eighteen caves and Rani Gupha with its victory-march relief and Yavana warrior; Cave 14, the Hathigumpha inscription and its three themes; the upasaka and pasanda vocabulary; the omission of the Mauryan invasion of Kalinga; Ganesh Gumpha and the Mancapuri lineage inscription; the Khandagiri caves; and the comparative judgement recorded between the two hills

### Visual: two hills, and one inscription that decides the history

```text

        mountain range                |

        most popular; DOUBLE-STOREYED |   twenty-four Jain Tirthankaras
        upper central wing: relief    |   carved on the walls;
        of a KING'S VICTORY MARCH;    |   Tirthankara Rishab Dev stands
        sculptures of a YAVANA        |   out
        warrior (Yavana = Greek       |
        speakers in ancient times)    |   TOTOWA GUMPHA - two adorned
        ------------------------------|   caves; two guards at the
        CAVE 14 = HATHIGUMPHA         |   entrance, two bulls and two
        a large NATURAL cave with     |   lions; parrots carved above
        the SEVENTEEN-LINE            |   the arch
        inscription of KHARAVELA      |
        ------------------------------|   ANANT GUMPHA - largest in
        GANESH GUMPHA - named for a   |   Khandagiri; carved arches,
        carved Ganesha; sculptures    |   pillars and panels; women,
        and reliefs                   |   elephants, athletes and geese
        ------------------------------|   carrying flowers
        MANCAPURI GUMPHA -            |
        double-storeyed; THREE        |   RECORDED COMPARISON: as
        inscriptions, one describing  |   against Khandagiri, the
        Kharavela's family and        |   UDAYAGIRI group offers more
        lineage                       |   beautiful and better
                                      |   maintained cave shrines

        PATRONAGE SPAN
        Kharavela, MAHA-MEGHAVAHANA dynasty, FIRST CENTURY BC
              -> rock-cutting continued to the SOMAVAMSI dynasty,
                 which ruled from the 9TH TO 12TH CENTURY AD
```

⚠️ Notice what the diagram makes visible: a single religious tradition holding one site across more than a thousand years of changing dynasties. That is a different pattern from Ellora, and the difference is the analytical point.

### 1. The site and its span

✅ The development of the present form of the **Udayagiri and Khandagiri Caves** in **Odisha** is said to be **majorly due to the contribution of Kalinga King Kharavela of the Maha-Meghavahana dynasty during the first century BC**. ✅ However, rock-cutting activities continued there until the time of the **Somavamsi dynasty, which ruled from the 9th to the 12th century AD**. ✅ The group is situated on **two adjacent hills**, located near the modern-day city of **Bhubaneswar**, and there are **more than 30 caves in the sandstone hillside, some man-made and some natural**.

✅ The **Udayagiri** cave complex has **around 18 caves** and was constructed by **King Kharavela, who followed Jainism in his personal life**; the Jain monks were given a place to dwell and meditate in these caves, erected on the **Kumari mountain range**. ✅ The **Khandagiri** group, on the adjacent hill, has **around 15 caves** in total.

⚠️ The comparison with Ellora writes itself and should be made explicitly in any coexistence answer. ⚠️ Ellora is **one site, three traditions, successive dynasties**. ⚠️ Udayagiri-Khandagiri is **one site, one tradition, successive dynasties** - Jain from Kharavela in the first century BC to the Somavamsi period more than a millennium later. ⚠️ Together they show that continuity of sanctity is the constant, while continuity of *sect* is variable, which is a far more precise statement than any generalisation about tolerance.

### 2. The caves worth naming

✅ **Rani Gupha** is the largest and most popular cave at Udayagiri and is **double-storeyed**. ✅ The upper portion of its central wing has **relief images depicting the victory march of a king**, and the cave also has sculptures of a **Yavana warrior**, the word Yavana meaning Greek speakers in ancient times. ✅ **Ganesh Gumpha** is named for its carved figure of Ganesha and is famous for sculptures and reliefs. ✅ The **Mancapuri Gumpha** is **double-storeyed**, and there are **three inscriptions** there, one of which describes the **family and lineage of King Kharavela**.

✅ At Khandagiri, the **Trushula Gumpha** has the **twenty-four Jain Tirthankaras carved on the cave walls**, with the sculpture of Tirthankara **Rishab Dev** standing out. ✅ **Totowa Gumpha** comprises two beautifully adorned caves with **two guards at the entrance, two bulls and two lions**, and **parrots carved above the entrance arch**. ✅ **Anant Gumpha** is the **largest cave in Khandagiri**, known for beautifully carved arches, pillars and panels, with sculptures of **women, elephants, athletes and geese carrying flowers**. ✅ As compared with Khandagiri, the **Udayagiri group offers more beautiful and better maintained cave shrines**.

⚠️ Two of these details are quietly important. ⚠️ The **Yavana warrior** at Rani Gupha is a second instance, alongside the Bharhut Yavana relief of Lesson 9, of a foreign figure represented in Indian religious art of this period - two independent sites, one phenomenon, which is exactly what a cultural-contact answer needs. ⚠️ And the **secular subjects** at Anant Gumpha - women, elephants, athletes, geese - repeat the Ajanta observation that religious monuments carry everyday material, and therefore serve as sources for social history.

### 3. The Hathigumpha inscription, and the most valuable silence in the topic

✅ The next important cave in the Udayagiri group is **Cave 14**, known as **Hathigumpha**. ✅ It is a **large natural cave** with a historically very important **seventeen-line inscription** narrating the life and achievements of the Jain king Kharavela. ✅ The inscription was carved in the **Prakrit language and in the Brahmi script** and is the **only piece of evidence about this Kalinga ruler**. ✅ The text of the seventeen-line inscription **starts with the Jain Namokar mantra** and thereafter focuses on **three main themes**: Kharavela's **military achievements**, his **benevolent activities**, and his being an **ardent follower and patron of Jainism**. ✅ In the inscription, Kharavela is described as a **layman or upasaka** devoted to Jain worship who at the same time respects all religious sects, indicated by the mention of the term **pasandas**. ✅ One point to note is that the Hathigumpha inscription **completely ignores mentioning the Mauryan invasion of the Kalinga region**.

⚠️ **Why this is the most instructive single object in the session.** Four things are true of it simultaneously, and holding all four is what an examiner is testing.

| ✅ What is true | ⚠️ What follows |
|---|---|
| It is the **only** piece of evidence about Kharavela | The reign is known through a single interested source, so every statement about it inherits that source's purpose |
| It opens with the **Namokar mantra** and records him as a patron of Jainism | Religious affiliation is explicit, and the monument's sectarian attribution rests on the text, not on the architecture |
| It describes him as an **upasaka** who respects all sects, using the term **pasandas** | The inscription itself claims a breadth of respect - which is a claim made by the patron, not an independent observation |
| It **omits the Mauryan invasion of Kalinga entirely** | A commemorative royal text records what serves its purpose; omission is selection, not ignorance |

⚠️ The disciplined sentence for an answer: the Hathigumpha inscription is exceptionally valuable because it is the principal narrative source for its subject, and exceptionally instructive because its silence on a major earlier event shows that royal epigraphy records what a patron wished remembered. ❌ What must not be written is that the omission proves Kharavela did not know of the invasion, or that it was deliberately suppressed for a specific stated motive; the evidence supports selection, and the motive is unrecorded.

⚠️ Set this against the Baroda grant of Lesson 14 and the pair completes the epigraphy lesson of the whole session: **one inscription corroborates a building, the other omits an event, and both are evidence of intention.**

### 4. What the site contributes to the Jain story

⚠️ Udayagiri-Khandagiri is the excavated half of the Jain tradition, and it establishes three things that the next lesson builds on. ⚠️ First, that Jain patronage included royal patronage of a scale capable of a large cave complex. ⚠️ Second, that the Jain use of excavated architecture was **residential and meditative** - the caves were given to monks to dwell and meditate in - rather than congregational, which distinguishes the Jain cave programme from the Buddhist chaitya-and-vihara pairing. ⚠️ Third, that a Jain site could carry an extensive **secular and narrative** relief programme, including a royal victory march, which is not what a reader expecting ascetic austerity would predict.

✅ The site is **not** a UNESCO World Heritage Site. ⚠️ That negative fact is worth holding precisely because the other major sites in this session are inscribed, and an objective item can be built from the assumption that every famous cave group is listed.

### Examination use

**Where it sits.** ✅ This is the topic's principal Jain cave case, its best inscription case and its clearest example of single-sect continuity at one site.

**Probable framing.** ⚠️ Inscriptions as historical evidence; Jain art and architecture; regional cave traditions; religious coexistence compared across sites.

**Traps.**

1. ❌ Treating the Hathigumpha inscription as a neutral, complete record. ⚠️ It is Kharavela's own commemorative text and omits the Mauryan invasion of Kalinga.
2. ❌ Getting the language or script wrong. ✅ Prakrit language, Brahmi script, seventeen lines, Cave 14.
3. ❌ Calling Udayagiri-Khandagiri a UNESCO site.
4. ⚠️ Confusing this Udayagiri with the Gupta-period Udayagiri caves near Vidisha in Madhya Pradesh, taught in Lesson 17.
5. ❌ Assigning the whole complex to one reign. ✅ Kharavela in the first century BC, with rock-cutting continuing to the Somavamsi period of the 9th-12th centuries.

### Revision notes

1. ✅ **Udayagiri and Khandagiri**, **Odisha**, twin adjacent hills near **Bhubaneswar**; **more than 30 caves** in the sandstone hillside, some man-made and some natural.
2. ✅ Principal patron: **Kharavela**, **Maha-Meghavahana** dynasty, **first century BC**, a Jain in his personal life; rock-cutting continued to the **Somavamsi** dynasty (**9th-12th century AD**).
3. ✅ **Udayagiri**: about **18 caves**, on the **Kumari mountain range**. **Khandagiri**: about **15 caves**.
4. ✅ **Rani Gupha**: largest and most popular, **double-storeyed**; relief of a **king's victory march**; **Yavana warrior** sculptures.
5. ✅ **Cave 14 = Hathigumpha**: a large **natural** cave with the **seventeen-line** inscription of Kharavela, in **Prakrit** and **Brahmi**; the **only** evidence about this ruler.
6. ✅ The inscription opens with the **Jain Namokar mantra**; three themes - **military achievements, benevolent activities, patronage of Jainism**; Kharavela described as an **upasaka** respecting all sects, with the term **pasandas**.
7. ✅ It **completely omits the Mauryan invasion of Kalinga** - the standard example of evidentiary silence as selection, not ignorance.
8. ✅ **Ganesh Gumpha** (carved Ganesha, sculptures and reliefs); **Mancapuri Gumpha** (double-storeyed; **three inscriptions**, one on Kharavela's family and lineage).
9. ✅ **Khandagiri**: **Trushula Gumpha** (24 Tirthankaras; **Rishab Dev** prominent); **Totowa Gumpha** (two guards, two bulls, two lions, parrots above the arch); **Anant Gumpha** (largest there; women, elephants, athletes, geese carrying flowers).
10. ✅ Recorded comparison: **Udayagiri** offers more beautiful and better maintained cave shrines than Khandagiri.
11. ⚠️ Pattern: one site, **one tradition**, successive dynasties - against Ellora's one site, three traditions.
12. ✅ Not a UNESCO World Heritage Site. ⚠️ Not to be confused with the Gupta-period Udayagiri caves near Vidisha.

### Concept check

**Question:** The Hathigumpha inscription is called both the best and the most dangerous source in this topic. Justify both descriptions in a single argument.

**Model answer:** It is the best because it is the only piece of evidence about Kharavela at all: a seventeen-line text in Prakrit and Brahmi, cut in a large natural cave known as Hathigumpha, Cave 14 at Udayagiri, opening with the Jain Namokar mantra and organised around his military achievements, his benevolent activities and his patronage of Jainism, and describing him as an upasaka who respects all sects, using the term pasandas. No other source supplies any of that, so the reign is recoverable only from it. It is the most dangerous for the same reason. A single interested source has no corroboration, and this one demonstrably selects: it completely omits the Mauryan invasion of the Kalinga region, an event of the first importance to the territory it describes. Since the omission cannot be attributed to ignorance, it shows that a royal commemorative inscription records what serves its patron's purpose. The correct handling is therefore to use the text for everything it states, to attribute every such statement to the inscription rather than to history at large, and to cite the silence as the standard illustration that epigraphy is interested testimony.

**Misconception to avoid:** Concluding that because the inscription omits an event, it is unreliable or propagandistic in a way that discredits it. The inference is narrower and more useful: royal inscriptions are highly reliable about what they choose to record and systematically silent about what does not serve the commemoration, and knowing which of the two you are looking at is the whole skill.

## Lesson 16 - Jain heritage beyond the caves: three scales of building

Progress: 16/18 | Stage: Core | Subtopic: the Jain tradition at three scales - excavated cell, dressed-stone temple and temple-city on a sacred hill; Dilwara and Ranakpur as the marble cases; Shravanabelagola with the Bahubali colossus, the twelve-yearly Mahamastakabhisheka and its Nayaka-period wall paintings; Palitana and Shatrunjaya, Shikharji and Girnar; Badami Cave 4 and the Khajuraho Jain group; the shared-idiom limitation; and the strict prohibitions that govern what may be claimed
