⚠️ Three conclusions follow from the routing evidence above, and all three should change preparation rather than reduce it.

1. ⚠️ **The objective load is heavier than the Mains load.** One directly owned Mains demand sits against three routed objective demands in the 2018-2023 span, one in 2026 and five further items from other examinations conducted by the same commission. ⚠️ That distribution is why this session invests so heavily in identification discriminators, exact vocabulary and part-and-function pairs.
2. ⚠️ **Identification is its own demand family.** ⚠️ Three of the objective items above are identification or pairing items rather than concept items. ⚠️ They are defeated by the method of Lesson 13 - pair a site with the single feature unique to it - and not by general familiarity.
3. ⚠️ **The Mains contribution is often indirect.** ⚠️ The lion-and-bull demand, the early-Buddhist-stupa-art demand and the Sarnath-pillar demand are owned elsewhere or shared, and in each case this topic supplies the monument evidence that the owning topic's answer needs. ⚠️ A candidate who treats a light direct Mains load as permission to skip the topic will meet its material inside somebody else's question.

# CUMULATIVE CONCEPT CHECKS

These run across lesson blocks rather than within a single lesson. Attempt each from memory before reading the model answer; the misconception line names the error that most often costs marks in that block.

## Block A - the Mauryan programme, Lessons 1 to 6

### Cumulative check A1 - one state, four kinds of surviving evidence

**Question:** Reconstruct what can be securely said about Mauryan building from four different kinds of surviving evidence, naming the evidence type in each case.

**Model answer:** The four kinds are excavated remains, standing objects, inscriptions on those objects and technical attributes. From excavated remains: the capital at Pataliputra is known archaeologically through the sites at Kumrahar and Bulandibagh near present-day Patna, where the remains of a palace were recovered and reconstructed as a large multi-storeyed timber structure with a high central pillar and carved wall decoration; because wood was the principal building material, this is reconstruction rather than description, and the traveller Megasthenes' judgement that the palace was among the greatest creations of mankind is testimony to impression rather than evidence of form. From standing objects: the pillars are monolithic shafts of Chunar sandstone averaging about forty feet, surmounted by a lotus- or bell-shaped capital, an abacus and an animal, with the bell form and the lustrous polish recorded as showing Iranian influence. From inscriptions: three cave inscriptions of Ashoka's time in the Barabar hills record dedications to Ajivika ascetics, the Nagarjuni caves record the same by Dasharatha, the Major Pillar Edicts survive at seven locations with only Delhi-Topra carrying all seven, and the Schism Edict is recorded at Sanchi, Sarnath and Allahabad. From technical attributes: the highly finished glossy polish appears on the Ashokan pillars, in the Barabar caves and on sculpture such as the Didarganj Yakshi, the Lohanipur torso, the Masarh lion and the Diamond throne of Bodhgaya, and it is a proxy for expenditure because polishing hard stone to that finish is an enormous investment of skilled labour.

**Misconception to avoid:** Assembling these four into a single confident portrait of "Mauryan architecture". They have different strengths: the excavated palace is modelled, the pillars are measurable, the inscriptions are self-testifying, and the polish is diagnostic but not exclusive. An answer that keeps them apart can say what is known and how; an answer that fuses them says more than the evidence allows.

### Cumulative check A2 - why the court-and-popular binary is both necessary and insufficient

**Question:** Defend the court-and-popular classification and then break it, using only Mauryan-period evidence.

**Model answer:** The defence is that the classification reflects something the record actually shows. As the Mauryas established their power a clear demarcation appears between architecture and sculpture developed under state patronage and that developed by individual initiative, and the two groups differ in format and audience: court art consists of works commissioned by the rulers for political and religious reasons, principally the palaces at Pataliputra and the pillars, which are standardised, enormously expensive, text-bearing and addressed to a dispersed public, while the popular group covers cave architecture, sculpture and pottery produced by individual effort. The break is at Barabar. The oldest known rock-cut architecture in India, in Jehanabad district, dates to the third century BC and was dedicated by Ashoka and his grandson Dasharatha to ascetics of the Ajivika sect, with inscriptions in the caves naming King Piyadassi and Devanampiya Dasaratha - so the ruling house itself financed what the classification files under individual initiative. A second break runs through technique: Mauryan polish appears on the imperial pillars, on the Barabar cave interiors and on free-standing sculpture alike, so whatever the classification separates, it does not separate workshops. The correct conclusion is that the binary is a classification of format and audience, not a map of funding.

**Misconception to avoid:** Concluding that a classification which breaks should be abandoned. The classification is the structure of a Mauryan answer; the break is the judgement. A script that supplies only the first is a summary, and one that supplies only the second has nothing to be critical of.

## Block B - stupas and their accretion, Lessons 7 to 10

### Cumulative check B1 - three stupas, three different arguments

**Question:** Assign Sanchi, Bharhut and Amaravati each a distinct analytical job, and state the evidence that makes each fit its job.

**Model answer:** Sanchi's job is accretion. Its fabric separates into a Mauryan brick nucleus crowned by a chatra, a Sunga expansion to twice the size with sandstone casing, a circumambulatory path, a vedika, a harmika and a three-tiered chattra for the three Jewels, Satavahana-period toranas in the four cardinal directions each of two pillars and three lintels, and Gupta-period additions comprising a victory inscription of Chandragupta II on the railings and Temple No. 17; the Siri-Satakarni inscription recording the gift of one top lintel of the southern torana by the artisans of Satakarni II both dates a phase and identifies the donor world. Bharhut's job is to supply a single-period control: it is recorded as a Shunga-period stupa in Satna district characterised by early narrative reliefs, yaksha-yakshi figures and aniconic representation of the Buddha, with the northern gateway as its finest work and a dated relief, the Bharhut Yavana, of about 100 BCE. Amaravati's job is elaboration plus afterlife: near the Krishna in Guntur district, anciently Dhanyakataka or Dharanikota, its Mahachaitya is believed to have been originally about twenty-seven metres tall, with a vedika, four toranas and ayaka pillars typically five to a platform at the four cardinal directions; a stupa believed built earlier under Ashoka was enlarged by the Satavahanas with limestone railings and narrative panels; its Prakrit inscriptions in Brahmi record patronage patterns; and Mackenzie's visit of 1797, Elliot's excavation of 1845 with the marbles now in the British Museum and Burgess's record of 1887 make its modern history part of its history.

**Misconception to avoid:** Using all three sites to make the same point about early Buddhist art. If the three are interchangeable in an answer, none of them is doing analytical work, and the script has become a list with three examples instead of an argument with three kinds of evidence.

### Cumulative check B2 - the fabric-and-survival argument in one chain

**Question:** Construct a single chain from the fabric of an early stupa to the apparent explosion of Buddhist art in the post-Mauryan period, naming the mechanism at each step.

**Model answer:** The chain has five links. First, the early stupa's fabric concentrates permanence in the wrong places for the historian: an unburnt-brick core carries no information, the burnt-brick skin and thick plaster are a finish, and the sculptures on the medhi and the toran were wooden. Second, wood does not survive, so the earliest decorative programmes are invisible and the record effectively begins when patrons could render furniture in stone - the same material-selection bias that makes the timber palace at Pataliputra a reconstruction rather than a description. Third, in the post-Mauryan period stupas became larger and more decorative and stone was increasingly used in place of wood and brick, with the Shungas introducing the decorated gateway. Fourth, a stone railing and gateway can carry a sustained relief programme, which is why narrative art appears at scale in exactly this period - Bharhut's early narrative reliefs, Sanchi's lintels with Jataka tales and scenes from the Buddha's life, Amaravati's greenish-white limestone narrative panels. Fifth, the apparent explosion is therefore partly a real increase in expenditure and elaboration under several regional patrons and partly a change in what survives, and an answer that states both is more accurate than one that credits either alone.

**Misconception to avoid:** Reading the post-Mauryan flowering as evidence that Buddhism suddenly became more popular. What the evidence shows is a change in medium and in patronage capacity; popularity is a separate claim needing separate evidence, and the material held here does not supply it.

## Block C - the cave tradition, Lessons 11 to 15

### Cumulative check C1 - one technique, five centuries, four religions

**Question:** Trace the excavated form from Barabar to Ellora, stating at each stage who used it and what changed, and end with the general conclusion the sequence supports.

**Model answer:** At Barabar and Nagarjuni in the third century BC, excavation is a royal act producing a handful of granite chambers with plain, highly polished interiors and echo effects, dedicated by Ashoka and Dasharatha to Ajivika ascetics, with the Lomas Rishi facade reproducing a wood-and-thatch cottage in stone. In the post-Mauryan period the form divides in two: viharas, already developed under the Mauryas, for residence, and chaitya halls, developed now, for assembly, with open courtyards and stone screen walls, and the developed rock-cut chaitya taking an apsidal, colonnaded, vaulted form with a stupa at the terminal end. In the western Deccan of the Satavahana period the technique becomes a repeatable institutional practice on the Ghats trade corridors - Karle, Bhaja, Nasik, Bedsa, Kondane, Pitalkhora, with Junnar, Kanheri and Kuda recorded as simple viharas - and Nasik's twenty-three caves add rock-cut water tanks and a dated dedication of Cave 3 to the Sangha in the reign of Vasishthiputra Pulumavi. At Ajanta the form supports two campaigns, five Satavahana-period caves of the Hinayana phase with a stupa as the object of worship and twenty-four Vakataka-period caves of the Mahayana phase, in which a shrine room is introduced into the vihara and Cave 26 shows a Buddha carved in high relief on a stupa. At Udayagiri-Khandagiri it serves a Jain royal programme of dwelling and meditation from Kharavela in the first century BC to the Somavamsis of the ninth to twelfth centuries. At Ellora it serves three religions on one hillside, ending with an entire temple carved from a single rock. The general conclusion is that a technique invented for one sect's ascetics became religiously neutral infrastructure, available to any community with the means and practically impossible to remove once made.

**Misconception to avoid:** Narrating this as a story of continuous progress towards Ellora. The stages are separated by centuries and by different patrons in different regions; what develops is scale, institutional depth and the range of users, not a single evolving style with a destination.

### Cumulative check C2 - two inscriptions, two lessons in evidence

**Question:** Compare the Baroda copper-plate grant and the Hathigumpha inscription as historical evidence, and state the single rule that both support.

**Model answer:** The Baroda inscription of 812 AD, a copper-plate grant of Karkaraja II, mentions Elapura - Ellora - by reference to the Kailasa temple built by Krishnaraja, recording his patronage of the Kailasanatha temple and describing it as a wonder that astonished even celestial beings and the architect. Its value is unusual: a text that corroborates a surviving building and its patron rather than merely commemorating a ruler, which is why it is cited alongside the monument itself in any account of Cave 16. Its limitation is that it is a eulogistic grant, so its rhetoric is praise, not description. The Hathigumpha inscription, cut in a large natural cave known as Cave 14 at Udayagiri, is a seventeen-line text in Prakrit and Brahmi, opening with the Jain Namokar mantra, organised around Kharavela's military achievements, benevolent activities and patronage of Jainism, and describing him as an upasaka who respects all sects; it is the only evidence about this ruler, and it completely omits the Mauryan invasion of the Kalinga region. Its value and its danger are the same fact: a single interested source with no corroboration. The rule both support is that inscriptions are evidence of intention - highly reliable about what a patron chose to record, and silent by design about what did not serve the commemoration - so the historian's first question of any inscription is not whether it is true but what it was for.

**Misconception to avoid:** Grading the two inscriptions as "reliable" and "unreliable". Both are reliable for what they state and both are shaped by purpose; the analytical move is to identify the purpose, not to rank the honesty.

## Block D - synthesis, Lessons 16 to 18

### Cumulative check D1 - what "coexistence" may and may not mean

**Question:** Three sites in this topic are used to argue about religious coexistence. Distinguish the claim each actually supports.

**Model answer:** Ellora supports successive and overlapping occupation of one hillside by three traditions: thirty-four caves divided into twelve Buddhist of the Mahayana and Vajrayana traditions, seventeen Hindu and five Jain of the Digambara sect, with the Buddhist group attributed to the Vakatakas and the Chalukyas, Cave 16 to Krishna I of the Rashtrakutas in the eighth century, Cave 21 to the Kalachuris and the Jain caves to the Yadavas, and inscriptions from the sixth century onwards - so the claim is competitive and successive patronage of a continuously sacred place, with phases that overlap rather than forming a clean relay. Udayagiri-Khandagiri supports something different: one site held by one tradition across changing dynasties, Jain from Kharavela of the Maha-Meghavahana dynasty in the first century BC through to the Somavamsis of the ninth to twelfth centuries, which is continuity of sect rather than plurality. Barabar supports a third claim again: a site created for Ajivika ascetics under royal endowment, which later received a Krishna statue dedicated by Anantvarman, a Hindu ruler of the Maukhari dynasty, and a Gupta-period inscription - reuse of a sacred place by a different religion centuries later, a pattern repeated at Mandapeshwar, which was developed as a Brahmanical cave and later converted into a Christian cave. Together they show that sanctity attaches to places and outlives the sect that first used them; none of them shows a society deliberately building a monument to pluralism.

**Misconception to avoid:** Treating the three as three proofs of one thesis. They support three different and more precise claims - simultaneous-looking succession, single-sect continuity and cross-religious reuse - and an answer that separates them demonstrates exactly the discrimination that the coexistence question is set to test.

### Cumulative check D2 - assembling a 20-mark answer from this topic

**Question:** Sketch the paragraph sequence of a 20-mark answer on rock-cut architecture as a source, naming the evidence each paragraph carries, without writing the answer itself.

**Model answer:** Seven movements. One, thesis: excavated monuments are unusually good evidence because they are immovable, datable by style and inscription and frequently inscribed with donor records, but they over-represent patrons who could afford permanence. Two, the origin and the counter-case: Barabar and Nagarjuni in Jehanabad district, third century BC, oldest known rock-cut architecture in India, dedicated by Ashoka and Dasharatha to Ajivika ascetics, with Lomas Rishi uninscribed and apparently unfinished - establishing royal endowment of a non-Buddhist sect and the limits of the court-and-popular classification. Three, form as evidence of liturgy: chaitya as assembly hall with a stupa at the terminal end against vihara as residence, with the shrine room later introduced into the vihara at Ajanta, showing a change of devotional practice readable from plan. Four, accretion: Sanchi's four phases with what each specifically added, and the Siri-Satakarni lintel inscription as evidence of a donor world of artisans. Five, distribution and economy: the western Deccan complexes on the Ghats corridors, Nasik's water tanks and its dated Cave 3 dedication, Amaravati on the Krishna in a trade centre active from the third century BC to the twelfth century AD. Six, epigraphy in both directions: the Baroda grant corroborating Kailasha, the Hathigumpha inscription omitting the Mauryan invasion of Kalinga. Seven, limitation and verdict: the material bias towards permanence, the inference-only status of proportion arguments such as Ellora's single chaitya among twelve Buddhist caves, and a closing judgement that answers the exact wording - that excavation documents what was endowed in stone and not the whole of religious life.

**Misconception to avoid:** Planning by site rather than by claim. A twenty-mark answer that moves Barabar, Sanchi, Ajanta, Ellora is a tour; one that moves thesis, origin, form, accretion, distribution, epigraphy, limitation is an argument that happens to visit the same places.

# ORIGINAL 10-, 15- AND 20-MARK MAINS MODEL PRACTICE

⚠️ These six questions are original to this session. ⚠️ Each carries a stated word ceiling, a complete model answer written inside that ceiling, and a scoring note that is specific to the question rather than a general instruction. ⚠️ Every model answer follows the same discipline: a claim, named evidence, analysis, and a qualification that survives scrutiny.

## Question 1 (10 marks, 150 words)

**Question:** Examine what the Barabar and Nagarjuni caves reveal about Mauryan religious patronage.

**Model answer:** The Barabar and Nagarjuni caves in Jehanabad district, Bihar, are the oldest known rock-cut architecture in India, cut in the third century BC. Their evidence is direct: three cave inscriptions of Ashoka's time in the Barabar hills record dedications to Ajivika ascetics, and the Nagarjuni inscriptions record the same by his grandson Dasharatha, naming King Piyadassi and Devanampiya Dasaratha. A Buddhist emperor therefore endowed permanent architecture for a non-Buddhist sect, showing that Mauryan patronage was broader than the ruler's own affiliation and that a monument's user is a separate question from its patron's belief. The caves also break the textbook division between court and popular art, since the ruling house financed what that classification assigns to individual initiative. The qualification is that architecture alone proves none of this: the chambers are plain, and the attribution is known only because the inscriptions were cut in the same rock.

⚠️ **Scoring note for this question.** The mark is won on the Ajivika dedication and lost on vagueness about who dedicated what. A script that names Ashoka and Dasharatha, the Ajivika sect and the inscriptional basis has the substance; one that writes "Ashoka built Buddhist caves at Barabar" has made two errors in seven words. The final sentence is the discriminator between a good and an average script, because it identifies *why* we know the affiliation.

## Question 2 (10 marks, 150 words)

**Question:** Discuss how the plan of a rock-cut chaitya hall reflects the ritual it was built to serve.

**Model answer:** A chaitya is a hall for assembly around an object of worship, as distinct from a vihara, the residence for monks that had already developed under the Mauryas. Its developed rock-cut form follows from circumambulation. The object of worship is a stupa placed at the terminal end, so the hall reproduces indoors the relationship that the open-air monument creates outdoors, where a vedika defines a pradakshina patha. The apsidal end lets the circuit run continuously behind the stupa instead of turning corners; two rows of pillars divide the hall into a nave and side aisles, so the aisles are the circumambulatory path brought indoors; and the vaulted ceiling reproduces a timber hall's curved roof, as on the Lomas Rishi facade. The qualification is that form varies: the core account describes chaitya halls generally as quadrangular chambers with flat roofs, so function is the constant and plan is not.

⚠️ **Scoring note for this question.** This question rewards explanation over description, and the single sentence that separates a first-class script is the one identifying the colonnade as the circumambulatory path taken indoors. Reversing chaitya and vihara is fatal at any length. Candidates who cannot recall the recorded variation in plan should still open with function, because the function statement is where the definitional mark sits.

## Question 3 (15 marks, 250 words)

**Question:** "A single monument can be a record of several political orders." Examine this statement with reference to the Great Stupa at Sanchi.

**Model answer:** Sanchi supports the statement because its phases are physically separable and are corroborated by inscriptions on the monument itself. The Mauryan phase is a brick core: the nucleus of Stupa no. 1 was a simple hemispherical brick structure crowned by a chatra, built in Ashoka's reign, the site's early prominence connected with his wife Devi, daughter of a merchant of nearby Vidisha. The Sunga phase converted that mound into a stone monument with a working ritual circuit - expanded to twice its size, cased in sandstone slabs, and given a circumambulatory path, a stone vedika and a harmika, with a three-tiered chattra above representing the Buddha, the Dhamma and the Sangha; Stupas 2 and 3 appear to belong to the same period, the relics of Sariputra and Mahamoggallana being associated with Stupa 3. The Satavahana phase, between the first century BC and the second century AD, added four toranas in the cardinal directions, each of two pillars supporting three richly carved lintels showing Jataka tales and scenes from the Buddha's life. The Gupta phase added a victory inscription of Chandragupta II on the railings and Temple No. 17, an early flat-roofed shrine. On the same hill stands an Ashokan lion-capital pillar of about 250 BC carrying a Schism Edict and an undeciphered Sankha Lipi text. Two qualifications hold: the Siri-Satakarni inscription records artisans gifting one lintel of the southern gateway, not a royal commission, and what a visitor sees reflects survey from 1851, princely funding and Marshall's restoration of 1912-19.

⚠️ **Scoring note for this question.** Marks concentrate on separability - what each phase specifically added - rather than on a chronology of dynasties. The two closing qualifications are where the top band is decided: the donor-world correction and the conservation-history correction are both available from the same evidence and both are routinely omitted. A script that attributes the toranas to a named king forfeits the attribution mark however good the rest is.

## Question 4 (15 marks, 250 words)

**Question:** Analyse the claim that Jain architecture in India is best identified by its programme rather than by a distinct style.

**Model answer:** The claim is well founded, and the evidence runs in both directions. Against a distinct style: the Khajuraho complex contains both Hindu and Jain temples sharing the same artwork vocabulary, and the five Digambara caves at Ellora, caves 30 to 34 including Chhota Kailash, Indra Sabha and Jagannath Sabha, share the excavation technique of the Buddhist and Brahmanical caves on the same hillside. Jain patrons therefore commissioned in the regional idiom available to them. For a distinct programme: the tradition builds at three scales that together are not paralleled elsewhere. The excavated cell serves ascetic residence and meditation - at Udayagiri-Khandagiri, where Kharavela of the Maha-Meghavahana dynasty gave monks a place to dwell and meditate in the first century BC and work continued to the Somavamsi period, and at Badami Cave 4, dedicated to the tirthankaras with sculptures of Parsvanath, Mahavir and Bahubali. The dressed-stone temple serves image worship, with Dilwara at Mount Abu and Ranakpur in Rajasthan as the named cases. The temple-city on a sacred hill serves circuit pilgrimage rather than congregation, at Palitana on the Shatrunjaya hills, the holiest Jain tirtha associated with Rishabhanatha, and at Shikharji on the Parasnath Range. Ritual is built into the fabric: the twelve-yearly Mahamastakabhisheka at Shravanabelagola requires the 57 ft colossus of Bahubali, son of Rishabhdev, and its approach. The qualification is evidential: the sources support location, function and patronage but not dates, architects or dimensions, so the programme argument is the one the evidence can carry.

⚠️ **Scoring note for this question.** The examiner is testing whether a candidate can argue a negative - that no distinct style exists - without sounding dismissive of the tradition. The Khajuraho and Ellora evidence must be named, because the negative is otherwise an assertion. Writing that the Shravanabelagola figure is a tirthankara destroys credibility in a Jain answer; the figure is Bahubali, son of Rishabhdev.

## Question 5 (20 marks, 300 words)

**Question:** Critically examine the proposition that Indian cave complexes demonstrate religious coexistence.

**Model answer:** The proposition is defensible only in a precise form, and the evidence separates into three patterns that are usually merged. The first is successive and overlapping occupation. Ellora's thirty-four caves in the Sahyadri range divide into twelve Buddhist of the Mahayana and Vajrayana traditions, seventeen Hindu and five Jain of the Digambara sect; the Buddhist group is attributed to the Vakatakas and the Chalukyas, Cave 16, the monolithic Kailasha dedicated to Shiva, to Krishna I of the Rashtrakutas in the eighth century, Cave 21 to the Kalachuris and the Jain caves to the Yadavas, with inscriptions from the sixth century onwards and a Dantidurga inscription in Cave 15 recording that a Rashtrakuta ruler offered prayers there. Different dynasties endowed different traditions at a place already in use, and the phases overlap rather than forming a clean relay. The second pattern is single-sect continuity: Udayagiri and Khandagiri near Bhubaneswar were developed chiefly by Kharavela of the Maha-Meghavahana dynasty in the first century BC, and rock-cutting continued to the Somavamsi dynasty of the ninth to twelfth centuries, a Jain tradition holding one site across more than a millennium. Third, reuse across religions: Lomas Rishi at Barabar, cut for Ajivika ascetics, later received a Krishna statue dedicated by Anantvarman of the Maukhari dynasty, and Mandapeshwar, a late Gupta Brahmanical cave, was later converted into a Christian cave. The common factor is not tolerance as a doctrine but sanctity attaching to a place and outlasting the community that first used it, sustained by excavation's physical properties: extremely expensive to create, impossible to destroy. The critical qualification is that these sites evidence competitive and successive patronage of continuously sacred geography; they do not evidence a designed inter-faith project, and converting material plurality into proof of abstract harmony is the standard failure this question detects.

⚠️ **Scoring note for this question.** The directive is "critically examine", so a script that only assembles evidence for coexistence has answered half the question. The three-pattern structure is what earns the analytical marks, and the physical explanation - cheap to reuse, impossible to remove - is the observation that lifts a good script into the top band because it explains the pattern rather than restating it.

## Question 6 (20 marks, 300 words)

**Question:** "Inscriptions are the most valuable and the most treacherous evidence in early Indian art history." Evaluate with reference to the monuments of this period.

**Model answer:** Both halves are demonstrable from the same corpus. Inscriptions are the most valuable evidence because they are cut into the object they describe, so the record and the monument are one thing. The Barabar and Nagarjuni inscriptions establish that the Mauryan ruling house dedicated caves to Ajivika ascetics, an attribution the plain chambers could never have supplied. The Sanchi pillar carries Ashoka's Schism Edict, warning of punishment for dissent in the sangha, and the Schism Edict is recorded at Sanchi, Sarnath and Allahabad. The Siri-Satakarni inscription in Brahmi records the gift of one top lintel of the southern torana at Sanchi by the artisans of Satakarni II, identifying a donor world of craft groups. The Udayagiri caves near Vidisha carry Sanskrit inscriptions in Gupta characters dated in Gupta Samvat 82 and 106, naming Chandragupta II and, in Cave 7, the maker Virasena. The Baroda copper-plate grant of 812 AD names Elapura and records Krishnaraja's patronage of the Kailasa temple - a text corroborating a surviving building. Inscriptions are treacherous because each is a statement made for a purpose. The Baroda grant calls the temple a wonder that astonished even celestial beings, which is eulogy rather than description. The Hathigumpha inscription, seventeen lines in Prakrit and Brahmi, is the only evidence about Kharavela, opens with the Jain Namokar mantra, records his military achievements, benevolence and patronage of Jainism, describes him as an upasaka respecting all sects - and omits the Mauryan invasion of Kalinga entirely. A third danger is unexplained silence: the Rampurva bull-capital pillar carries no inscription and the sources give no reason, so none may be supplied. The resolution is that inscriptions are reliable about what a patron chose to record, and the first question is not whether the text is true but what it was for.

⚠️ **Scoring note for this question.** This question is graded on whether the candidate can hold "valuable" and "treacherous" together instead of choosing a side. At least three named inscriptions are required on each side, and the Rampurva silence is the detail that distinguishes a script that has thought about evidence from one that has memorised two examples. An answer that treats the Hathigumpha omission as proof of deliberate suppression overstates and loses the qualification mark.

# REMEDIATION

⚠️ Twenty-nine recurring errors, grouped into five families of five, five, six, seven and six entries. ⚠️ Each entry names the error, states the correction with its evidence, and identifies the lesson where the material is taught.

## Family 1 - Evidence-status errors

| ❌ Error | ✅ Correction | Lesson |
|---|---|---|
| Describing the Mauryan palace as though it had been observed | The palace at Kumrahar is a reconstruction from excavated remains; wood was the principal building material, and Megasthenes supplies a judgement, not a description | 2 |
| Treating an inference from proportion as a documented fact | Ellora's single chaitya among twelve Buddhist caves suggests an emphasis on residence; it is an inference, and a chaitya is far costlier than a cell | 11, 14 |
| Presenting a debated attribution as settled | Elephanta's caves are attributed by scholars to various Hindu dynasties; no single dynasty may be named as the sole patron | 17 |
| Supplying a reason for an unexplained absence | The Rampurva bull-capital pillar carries no inscription and the sources give no reason; the absence is recorded, not explained | 4 |
| Completing incomplete source text | The lists of notable post-Mauryan stupas and of notable post-Mauryan architecture are incomplete in the text available; only what is recorded is used | 9, 12 |

## Family 2 - Attribution and patronage errors

| ❌ Error | ✅ Correction | Lesson |
|---|---|---|
| "Satakarni II built the Sanchi gateways" | The Siri-Satakarni inscription records the gift of one top lintel of the southern torana by the artisans of that king; write "Satavahana-period" | 8 |
| "Chandragupta dedicated the Barabar caves" | Ashoka dedicated caves in the Barabar hills and Dasharatha in the Nagarjuni hills, both to Ajivikas | 6 |
| "Mauryan religious patronage was Buddhist patronage" | The oldest rock-cut architecture in India was endowed by the ruling house for the Ajivika sect | 1, 6 |
| "Satavahana-period means Satavahana royal funding" | Period labels describe political context; donors are identified by inscriptions, and recorded donors include artisans, guilds and individuals | 8, 12 |
| "Ellora was built by the Rashtrakutas" | Cave 16 is attributed to Krishna I of the Rashtrakutas; the Buddhist caves are attributed to the Vakatakas and the Chalukyas, Cave 21 to the Kalachuris and the Jain caves to the Yadavas | 14 |

## Family 3 - Terminology and form errors

| ❌ Error | ✅ Correction | Lesson |
|---|---|---|
| Interchanging chaitya and vihara | Chaitya = prayer and assembly hall; vihara = residence for monks; never reverse | 11 |
| Confusing abacus, capital and animal | Shaft, then capital (lotus or bell), then abacus (circular or rectangular), then the animal figure on the abacus | 3 |
| Confusing harmika with vedika | Harmika is the square railing at the summit of the anda; vedika is the railing at ground level defining the circuit | 7 |
| Treating the chhatra as structural | It is an umbrella of honour; at Sanchi a three-tiered chattra represents the Buddha, the Dhamma and the Sangha | 7, 8 |
| Assuming every capital has an abacus | Vaishali's single lion capital has none, which is why it is the clean contrast to Sarnath | 4 |
| Describing the pillar shaft as built of drums | The shaft is a monolith, a single piece of stone, of Chunar sandstone, averaging about 40 ft | 3 |

## Family 4 - Chronology, geography and attribution-of-place errors

| ❌ Error | ✅ Correction | Lesson |
|---|---|---|
| Placing Bagh in Maharashtra or in a gorge | Bagh is on the bank of the Baghni river, Dhar district, Madhya Pradesh | 17 |
| Attaching a river gorge to a site identified by another feature | The gorge identifier in this session's evidence belongs to Ajanta - the Waghora gorge in the Sahyadri hills, Aurangabad district; other sites carry their own identifiers | 10, 12, 13 |
| Confusing the two Udayagiris | Udayagiri near Vidisha is Gupta and Hindu; Udayagiri with Khandagiri in Odisha is Jain and Kharavela's | 15, 17 |
| Giving a clean Buddhist-then-Hindu-then-Jain sequence at Ellora | The groups broadly sequence but the phases overlap | 14 |
| Treating the Ajanta cave total as the analytical point | The source states 29 caves, 5 Satavahana-period and 24 Vakataka-period; argue from the division, since totals vary with what is counted | 13 |
| Misremembering inscription years | Ajanta 1983, Ellora 1983, Elephanta 1987, Sanchi 1989; Udayagiri-Khandagiri, Amaravati and Bharhut are not inscribed | 8, 10, 13, 14, 15 |
| Converting the book's approximate early-stupa dates into a precise chronology | Bharhut 150 BC, Sanchi Second 125 BC, Bodhgaya 75 BC, Great Stupa 50 BC, Sanchi Third 25 BC are the book's approximations | 9 |

## Family 5 - Interpretation and answer-writing errors

| ❌ Error | ✅ Correction | Lesson |
|---|---|---|
| "Ellora proves India's eternal religious harmony" | The evidence supports successive and overlapping patronage of a continuously sacred place, not a designed inter-faith project | 14, 18 |
| Treating the Hathigumpha omission as proof of suppression | Omission is selection by a commemorative text; the motive is unrecorded | 15 |
| Writing a site tour when the question asks about evidence | Every demand in this family rewards what the object proves over what it looks like | 18 |
| Stating a claim without its limitation | The limitation column is half the answer, and the master limitation - the bias towards permanent material - is the strongest closing qualification available | 18 |
| Calling the Shravanabelagola colossus a tirthankara | The figure is Bahubali, son of Rishabhdev, 57 ft; the "last tirthankara" description fails twice over | 16 |
| Claiming a distinct Jain architectural style | Khajuraho's shared vocabulary and Ellora's shared technique refute it; the distinctiveness is of programme and patron community | 16 |

# MASTER COMPARISON, CAUSAL AND ARGUMENT MAPS

## Map 1 - The chronology rail of the whole topic

```text
 3rd c. BC   MAURYAN
             Pataliputra: Kumrahar, Bulandibagh (excavated; timber palace)
             Ashokan pillars: Chunar sandstone, monolith, c. 40 ft
               Sarnath (4 lions, carved circular abacus, Dharmachakra)
               Vaishali/Kolhua (1 lion, NO abacus)
               Sanchi (4 lions, flatter abacus: geese + flame palmettes, c. 250 BC)
               Rampurva (lion pillar: Major Pillar Edicts I-VI; bull pillar: none)
             BARABAR + NAGARJUNI: oldest rock-cut architecture; AJIVIKA
             Stupas: Piprahwa (oldest); Deur Kothar; Sanchi brick core
                |
 185 BC      SHUNGA dynasty replaces the Mauryan empire
 2nd-1st c.  POST-MAURYAN: Shungas, Kanvas, Kushanas, Shakas (north);
   BC          Satavahanas, Ikshvakus, Abhiras, Vakatakas (south/west)
             Stupas larger, more decorative; STONE replaces wood/brick
             SHUNGAS introduce TORANAS
             BHARHUT (Satna, MP): narrative relief, yaksha-yakshi, ANICONIC
             SANCHI: Sunga doubling + stone casing + vedika + harmika
             CHAITYA hall develops (vihara already Mauryan)
             WESTERN DECCAN: Karle, Bhaja, Nasik, Bedsa, Kondane, Pitalkhora
                |
 1st c. BC   KHARAVELA (Maha-Meghavahana): UDAYAGIRI-KHANDAGIRI, Odisha
             HATHIGUMPHA: 17 lines, Prakrit + Brahmi
 1st c. BC - SATAVAHANA phase at SANCHI: four TORANAS
   2nd c. AD AMARAVATI: Mahachaitya enlarged; ayaka pillars; limestone relief
             NASIK Cave 3: Gautamiputra vihara, c. 150 CE
                |
 4th c. AD   GUPTA: mural painting added to caves; stupa building DECLINES
             (Dhamek at Sarnath = renovation); TEMPLE architecture emerges
             SANCHI: Chandragupta II victory inscription; TEMPLE 17 (flat roof)
             UDAYAGIRI near VIDISHA: Gupta Samvat 82 (401-02) and 106 (425-26)
 5th-6th c.  AJANTA phase II: VAKATAKA, Harishena (c. 460-477 CE), MAHAYANA
             BAGH (Baghni river, Dhar district); UNDAVALLI (Jain -> Vishnukundina)
 5th-8th c.  ELEPHANTA (attribution debated); JUNAGADH; MANDAPESHWAR
 8th c. AD   ELLORA Cave 16 KAILASHA: Krishna I, RASHTRAKUTA; Dantidurga
             inscription in Cave 15
 812 AD      BARODA copper-plate of Karkaraja II names ELAPURA
 9th-12th c. SOMAVAMSI continuation at Udayagiri-Khandagiri;
             Ellora's Jain caves (Yadava commission)
```

⚠️ Use the rail as a retrieval spine, not as a claim about smooth development. Its own gaps - the centuries between Ajanta's two campaigns, the distance between Barabar and the western Deccan - are part of what it teaches.

## Map 2 - The identification map, including two boundary sites

| Site | ✅ River, landform or island | ✅ Region and district | Unique discriminator |
|---|---|---|---|
| **Ajanta** | **Gorge of the Waghora river** | Sahyadri hills, **Aurangabad district, Maharashtra** | The horseshoe gorge; the district alone is not a discriminator |
| **Ellora** | Rock escarpment; no river identifier in the sources | Sahyadri range, Maharashtra, about 100 km from Ajanta | 34 caves across three religions; the Cave 16 monolith |
| **Bagh** | **Bank of the Baghni river** | **Dhar district, Madhya Pradesh** | River plus district; ❌ not a gorge, ❌ not Maharashtra |
| **Elephanta** | Island in the Arabian Sea | **Gharapuri**, about 11 km east of Mumbai | The island itself |
| **Amaravati** | Near the **Krishna river** | **Guntur district, Andhra Pradesh** | The ancient names **Dhanyakataka / Dharanikota** |
| **Undavalli** | Hillside overlooking the **Krishna river** | Guntur district, Andhra Pradesh, south-west of Vijayawada | Four storeys, monolithic |
| **Barabar and Nagarjuni** | Granite hills | **Jehanabad district, Bihar** | Oldest known rock-cut architecture; Ajivika dedication |
| **Udayagiri-Khandagiri** | Twin adjacent hills | Odisha, near **Bhubaneswar** | The Hathigumpha inscription |
| **Udayagiri (Gupta)** | Rock-cut sanctuaries | Near **Vidisha, Madhya Pradesh** | Dated Gupta Samvat inscriptions naming Chandragupta II |
| **Nasik (Pandav Leni / Trirashmi Leni)** | Hill caves | Nasik, Maharashtra | 23 Buddhist caves; ❌ not a gorge site |
| **Junagadh** | Hill caves with a citadel | **Junagadh district, Gujarat** | The 30-50 ft "Upar Kot" citadel before the prayer hall |
| **Sanchi** | Hilltop complex | Madhya Pradesh; anciently **Kakanaya** | The Great Stupa with four toranas; ❌ no river identifier |
| **Bharhut** | — | **Satna district, Madhya Pradesh** | Shunga-period narrative relief and aniconic representation |
| **Besnagar** *(boundary site)* | — | **The site of ancient Vidisha, Madhya Pradesh** | ✅ The **Heliodorus pillar**, a 2nd-century BC **Garuda pillar** set up in honour of **Vasudeva (Vishnu)** by **Heliodorus**, an ambassador of **Antialcidas**, described in the source as the Greek king of Taxila, at the court of the Shunga prince **Bhagabhadra**; inscribed in **Prakrit** and **Brahmi**, and locally called **Khamba Baba** |
| **Sittanavasal** *(boundary site)* | — | **Tamil Nadu** | ✅ A **Jain** cave complex traceable from the 1st century BC to the 10th century AD, known for tirthankara sculptures and **mural paintings**, with the rock-cut cave temple called **Arivar Koil**; its paintings were patronised by the **Pandyas** |

⚠️ The last two rows are boundary identifications rather than sites taught in the lessons, and they are recorded because a routed objective item names them alongside a western Deccan site. ⚠️ Their fuller treatment - the Heliodorus pillar as a post-Mauryan inscription, and Sittanavasal as a painting site - belongs to the inscriptions and painting material of other topics.

## Map 3 - Function-and-form comparison across the excavated tradition

| Feature | Chaitya | Vihara | Structural stupa | Monolithic temple |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Purpose | Assembly around an object of worship | Residence for monks | Relic repository and commemoration | Image worship in a carved temple |
| Object at the centre | A stupa at the terminal end; later, an image | Cells around a hall; later, a shrine room | The relic within the anda | The shivalingam in the sanctum |
| Movement | Circumambulation along side aisles | Daily residence and meditation | Circumambulation on the pradakshina patha | Approach through mandapa to sanctum |
| Named example | Ajanta caves 9, 10, 19, 26, 29; Ellora Cave 10 | Ajanta Cave 1; Nasik Cave 3; Ellora caves 1-12 except one | Sanchi Stupa 1; Amaravati Mahachaitya | Ellora Cave 16, Kailasha |
| What it evidences | Congregational practice and its scale | Resident community and its size | Relic cult, donor wealth and accretion | Design capability and subtractive planning |

## Map 5 - Answer spines

⚠️ Four spines, each executable from memory in an examination hall.

```text
SPINE A - ROCK-CUT AS A SOURCE (10 marks)
thesis (immovable, datable, donor-recorded, but biased to permanence)
  -> Barabar: royal endowment of a NON-Buddhist sect
  -> chaitya/vihara: liturgy readable from plan
  -> Sanchi: accretion across four political orders
  -> limitation: perishable material invisible
  -> verdict answering the exact wording

SPINE B - EVOLUTION OF THE STUPA (15 marks)
origin (pre-Buddhist, not exclusively Buddhist) + function (relic repository,
votive, commemorative)
  -> anatomy with function (anda, medhi, vedika, patha, harmika, chhatra, torana)
  -> fabric (unburnt core, burnt skin, plaster, WOODEN furniture)
  -> post-Mauryan: larger, stone replaces wood, Shungas introduce toranas
  -> accretion at ONE named site: Sanchi, phase by phase
  -> regional elaboration: Amaravati's ayaka pillars and limestone relief
  -> status line (Sanchi 1989) -> verdict

SPINE C - COURT AND POPULAR ART (10-15 marks)
state the classification (commissioned by rulers vs individual initiative)
  -> court: Pataliputra palaces, the pillar programme
  -> popular: cave architecture, sculpture, pottery
  -> THE BREAK: Barabar-Nagarjuni are royal Ajivika dedications
  -> the second break: Mauryan polish crosses both categories
  -> verdict: a classification of format and audience, not of funding

SPINE D - JAIN ARCHITECTURE (15 marks)
material (living rock / dressed marble / monolithic colossus)
  -> form at three scales (cell, temple, temple-city on a hill)
  -> patronage (royal: Kharavela, early Chalukyas; mercantile/community: the hills)
  -> function built into fabric (12-yearly Mahamastakabhisheka; circuit pilgrimage)
  -> limitation (shared idiom at Khajuraho and Ellora)
  -> verdict: identified by programme, not by a separate architectural language
```

# COMPLETE CONSOLIDATED REGISTER NOTES

⚠️ These are compressed revision notes covering every lesson, organised by the topic's own structure rather than by a generic notes template. ⚠️ They assume the teaching above and do not repeat it.

## 1. Evidence grammar for this topic

- Four statuses: **monument fact** (what the object is), **attribution** (who made it, on what authority), **reconstruction** (what is gone but modelled), **inference** (what is read from proportion or distribution).
- Evidence hierarchy: a direct inscription on the object is strongest for what it states; a foreign traveller's account is corroboration filtered through the observer; an attribution without source support is weak.
- Master limitation: excavated and stone monuments over-represent patrons who could afford permanence; timber, thatch and brick domestic building is invisible.
- Inscriptions record intention: reliable for what a patron chose to record, silent by design on what did not serve the commemoration.

## 2. Mauryan court architecture

- Capital **Pataliputra** (present-day Patna, Bihar); excavated sites **Kumrahar** and **Bulandibagh**; remains of a palace at Kumrahar.
- **Wood** was the principal building material; the excavated Pataliputra capital is said to show Greek influence in Indian art; Chandragupta Maurya's palace is stated to be inspired by the **Achaemenid palaces at Persepolis**.
- **Megasthenes**: the palace among the greatest creations of mankind - testimony to impression.
- **Ashoka's palace at Kumrahar**: massive, high central pillar, three storeys, timber, carved and sculpted walls - a reconstruction.
- Court art = works commissioned by rulers for political and religious reasons; popular art = cave architecture, sculpture, pottery by individual effort. The division is of format and audience, not of funding.

## 3. The Ashokan pillar

- Purposes: symbol of the state; commemoration of victories; propagation of imperial sermons.
- **Chunar sandstone**; average **c. 40 ft**; **monolithic shaft**; **lotus- or bell-shaped capital**; **abacus** (circular or rectangular); **animal figure**.
- **Iranian influence** is stated for the **bell capital** and the **lustrous polish** only.
- **F. O. Oertel** excavated the Lion Capital in **1904-05**.
- **Sarnath**: four lions back-to-back, **circular abacus** with bull, horse, lion, elephant and the **Dharmachakra**. National Emblem = abacus + animal part, adopted **26 January 1950**; **Satyameva Jayate** from the **Mundaka Upanishad**, in Devanagari, below the abacus; crowned by the Wheel of the Law; horse west, elephant east, bull south, lion north; associations - Queen Maya's dream, Taurus, Kanthaka, enlightenment; commemorates **Dharmachakrapravartana**.
- **Vaishali (Kolhua)**: single lion, **no abacus**. **Sanchi**: four lions, **flatter abacus with geese and flame palmettes**, c. **250 BC**, carrying the **Schism Edict** and a Gupta **Sankha Lipi** text, undeciphered; **Prinsep** deciphered Brahmi. Schism Edicts at **Sanchi, Sarnath, Allahabad**.
- **Rampurva**: two pillars, lion and bull; the lion carries **Major Pillar Edicts I-VI**, the bull carries none and no reason is recorded.
- **Seven** Major Pillar Edict locations: Kandahar, Delhi-Topra, Delhi-Meerut, Allahabad, Lauriya-Araraj, Lauriya-Nandangarh, Rampurva; only **Delhi-Topra** has all seven edicts; **Edict VII** only there, testamentary; **Prakrit and Brahmi** everywhere except Afghanistan.
- **Minor Pillar Edicts** (Nepal): **Rummindei** - Lumbini visit, exemption from *bali*, *bhaga* cut from 1/6 to 1/8; **Nigali Sagar** - a stupa's height doubled.
- **Deur Kothar** (MP): six-line Brahmi inscription **believed to be** the earliest evidence of the historic Buddha.

## 4. Mauryan polish

- Highly finished, glossy, extraordinarily precise surfaces.
- Found on the **Barabar caves**, the **Didarganj Yakshi** and the **Ashokan pillars**; also the **Lohanipur torso** (Mauryan and Kushan periods), the **Masarh lion**, the **Diamond throne of Bodhgaya**.
- Abandoned after the Mauryan period, the recorded reason being **high cost** - one explanation, not a demonstrated sole cause.
- Characteristic, not exclusive; it cannot settle a date. The Didarganj Yakshi's date is debated.

## 5. Barabar and Nagarjuni

- **Jehanabad district, Bihar**; **oldest known** rock-cut architecture in India; **3rd century BC**; **four** caves at Barabar, **three** at Nagarjuni; granite; polished interiors; echo effects; two chambers in most; **no murals**.
- **Ajivika** dedications by **Ashoka** (Barabar) and **Dasharatha** (Nagarjuni); inscriptions name **"King Piyadassi"** and **"Devanampiya Dasaratha"**.
- **Lomas Rishi**: facade recreating a wood-and-thatch cottage, elephants along the curved cornice towards stupa emblems, **no Ashokan inscription** (apparently never completed after rock-slide problems), later Krishna dedication by **Anantvarman**, Maukhari.
- **Sudama**: two rooms, Ashoka's dedication. **Karan Chaupar**: single rectangular polished room, Gupta inscription **"Daridra Kantara"**. **Vishwakarma**: "steps of Ashoka", the only cave with no post-Ashokan historical inscriptions.
- Nagarjuni: **Gopika, Vadathi, Vapiya**, consecrated by Dasaratha. **Huein Tsang** visited, Patna to Gaya.

## 6. The stupa

- Tradition **pre-Buddhist**; **not exclusively Buddhist** in significance; generally a **repository of relics**; a **votive and commemorative** structure in the Buddhist tradition.
- **Nine** stupas after the Buddha's death; **eight** with relics at the **medhi**, the **ninth** with the pot; Rajagriha, Vaishali, Kapilavastu, Allakappa, Ramagrama, Vethapida, Pava, Kushinagar, Pippalivana.
- Fabric: **unburnt brick core**, **burnt brick outer surface**, **thick plaster**; **wooden sculptures** on medhi and toran.
- Parts and functions: **anda**, **medhi**, **vedika**, **pradakshina patha**, **harmika**, **chhatra/chhatravali**, **torana**; plus **vahalkada** (frontispiece at the four cardinal directions) and **kinjalkini** (garland of a thousand lotus flowers).
- **Piprahwa** (UP) is the oldest; **Deur Kothar** (MP) credited to Ashoka.
- Post-Mauryan: larger and more decorative; **stone replaces wood and brick**; the **Shungas introduce toranas**, described as evidence of Hellenistic influence.

## 7. Sanchi

- Anciently **Kakanaya**, Madhya Pradesh; material mainly **3rd century BC to 6th century AD**.
- **Mauryan** brick core of Stupa 1 under Ashoka, crowned by a chatra; **Devi** of **Vidisha**.
- **Sunga**: doubled, sandstone casing, path, **vedika**, **harmika**, **three-tiered chattra** for **Buddha, Dhamma, Sangha**; Stupas **2** and **3**; relics of **Sariputra and Mahamoggallana** in Stupa 3; Cunningham's **"The Bhilsa Topes"**.
- **Satavahana (1st c. BC - 2nd c. AD)**: four **toranas**, cardinal directions, each two pillars and three lintels, Jataka tales and Buddha-life scenes; **Siri-Satakarni inscription** records the gift of one top lintel of the **southern** torana by the **artisans** of Satakarni II; **Salabhanjika** bracket motif.
- **Gupta**: **Chandragupta II victory inscription** on the railings; **Temple No. 17**, earliest temple stage, flat roof.
- On site: Ashokan **lion-capital pillar**, c. 250 BC, Schism Edict plus undeciphered Sankha Lipi.
- Rediscovery and conservation: **Cunningham and Maisey, 1851**; **Shahjehan Begum** and **Sultan Jehan Begum**; **John Marshall, 1912-1919**. **UNESCO 1989**; on the ₹200 note.

## 8. Bharhut and the early narrative phase

- Post-Mauryan patrons: Shungas, Kanvas, Kushanas, Shakas (north); Satavahanas, Ikshvakus, Abhiras, Vakatakas (south and west). **Shunga dynasty replaced the Mauryan empire in 185 BCE**.
- **Bharhut** (Satna district, MP), Shunga: **early narrative reliefs**, **yaksha-yakshi** figures, **aniconic** Buddha; **northern gateway** the finest work; **Bharhut Yavana** relief of a warrior, **100 BCE**.
- **Bodhgaya railings** (Bihar, Shunga): stone railings and pillars, lotus and elephants.
- The book's approximate dates, reordered here into sequence: **Bharhut 150 BC; Sanchi Second 125 BC; Bodhgaya 75 BC; Great Stupa 50 BC; Sanchi Third 25 BC**.
- Shared motifs: **Shalabhanjika** at Sanchi and Bharhut, found in Hindu, Buddhist and Jain art; **Gajalakshmi** on Bharhut railings, a 3rd-century AD Kausambi coin, and the Sanchi vedika.
- Boundary: the symbol-to-image transition and the sculpture schools belong to the sculpture topic; this topic owns the architectural correlate.

## 9. Amaravati

- Near the **Krishna**, **Guntur district, Andhra Pradesh**; anciently **Dhanyakataka / Dharanikota**; **Mahayana** centre; trade centre **3rd century BC to 12th century AD**.
- **Mahachaitya**, **believed originally c. 27 metres** tall; stone **vedika**; **four toranas**; **ayaka pillars** at the four cardinal directions, typically **five** per platform, for **birth, renunciation, enlightenment, Dharmachakra Pravartana, Mahaparinirvana**.
- A stupa **believed built earlier by Ashoka**, **enlarged by the Satavahanas** with limestone railings, sculpted panels and gateways; **Prakrit** inscriptions in **Brahmi** recording socio-religious life and patronage patterns.
- **Mackenzie 1797**; **Elliot 1845** and the **Amaravati / Elliot marbles** in the **British Museum**; **Burgess 1887**; ASI conservation; a **replica** near the original location.
- Boundary note on the school: indigenous development, Ikshvaku continuation, narrative limestone relief, tribhanga, the abhayamudra Buddha replicated in South and South-East Asia, influence on later South Indian sculpture, related sites at **Bhattiprolu, Jaggayyapeta, Nagarjunakonda, Ghantasala, Goli** and **Ter**; **Hiuen Tsang** in **640 AD**.

## 10. Chaitya and vihara

- **Chaitya** = prayer and assembly hall; **vihara** = residence. Viharas developed under the **Mauryas**; chaitya halls developed in the **post-Mauryan** period.
- Developed rock-cut chaitya: apsidal end, stupa at the terminal end, colonnades forming aisles, vaulted roof - each element serving circumambulation and remembering a timber prototype.
- Recorded variation: the core account describes chaitya halls generally as **quadrangular chambers with flat roofs**. Lead with function.
- Caves had **open courtyards** and **stone screen walls** against rain; human and animal decoration.
- A **shrine room in the vihara** is an innovation of the later Ajanta phase.

## 11. The western Deccan

- Satavahana row: **Karle, Bhaja, Nasik, Bedsa, Kondane, Pitalkhora** (Maharashtra); second row **Junnar, Kanheri, Kuda** - "Simple Viharas", with an incomplete patron label that is not completed here.
- **Nasik**: **23** caves, **Pandav Leni / Trirashmi Leni**, perhaps **1st century BC to 3rd century AD**, **Hinayana** with later **Mahayana** influence; Buddha shown by **throne and footprints**, later by images; **water tanks cut from solid rock**; **Cave 3 = Gautamiputra vihara, c. 150 CE**, dedicated to the **Sangha** in the reign of **Vasishthiputra Pulumavi**.
- Distribution argument: caves cluster on the Ghats trade corridors, implying a mercantile donor world under Satavahana authority.

## 12. Ajanta

- **Gorge of the Waghora river**, **Sahyadri hills**, **Aurangabad district, Maharashtra**; **John Smith**, **1819**.
- **29** caves: **5** Satavahana-period, **24** Vakataka-period; **horse-shoe** arrangement; chaityas **9, 10, 19, 26, 29**, the rest viharas.
- Phase I **Hinayana**, object of worship a **stupa**; Phase II **Mahayana** under **Harishena (c. 460-477 CE)**; **Vakatakas contemporaries of the Imperial Guptas**.
- **Cave 1** the most sumptuous rock-cut vihara in India; **Cave 6** two-storeyed; **Cave 26** a huge stupa with a seated Buddha in high relief and a reclining **parinibbana** scene.
- Murals on **already dried surfaces**, **fresco secco**, **six colours**; subjects include Jatakas, Bodhisattvas, Yakshas, Gandharvas, Apsaras and everyday life; **Vessantara Jataka**, **Padmapani**, **Vajrapani**, **The Dying Princess** (Cave 16), **King Janaka and his Queen** (Cave 1), **The Temptation of Mara** (Cave 26).
- **Fa Hien** and **Hieun Tsang** refer to the caves; **UNESCO 1983**.

## 13. Ellora

- Sahyadri range, Maharashtra, about **100 km from Ajanta**; roughly **5th-11th centuries AD** in the core account; old name **Elloorpuram**.
- **34** caves: **12 Buddhist** (Mahayana and Vajrayana, 1-12), **17 Hindu** (13-29), **5 Jain** (Digambara, 30-34). Phases **overlap**.
- Buddhist caves attributed to the **Vakatakas and the Chalukyas**; **only one is a chaitya** - **Cave 10, Vishvakarma / Carpenter's Cave**, rock finished like **wooden beams**, seated Buddha in **vyakhyana mudra** before a votive stupa; **Cave 12 Tin Thal**, three storeys, **seven Manushi** and **seven Dhyani Buddhas**.
- **Cave 16 Kailasha**: monolithic, Shiva, **Krishna I, Rashtrakuta, 8th century**, believed the largest monolith Hindu temple in the world; two storeys, shivalingam, flat-roofed mandapa on **16 pillars**, **Dravidian shikhara**, two flagstaff pillars, Nandi mandapa; sculptures including Ravana shaking Mount Kailasha, the Shiva-Parvati wedding, Durga and Mahishasura, Nataraja, Lingodbhava, Andhakasuravadha, Kalyanasundara; **Atiriktanga-Bhairava** is a striking Ellora form.
- **Cave 15 Dashavatara** with the **Dantidurga** inscription; **Cave 21 Rameshwar Lena** (Kalachuri), Ganga and Yamuna at the entrance; **Cave 29 Dhumar Lena**. Inscriptions from the **6th century** onwards.
- Jain caves **30, 32, 33 = Chhota Kailash, Indra Sabha, Jagannath Sabha**, commissioned by the **Yadavas**.
- **Baroda inscription, 812 AD**, copper-plate grant of **Karkaraja II**, names **Elapura** and records **Krishnaraja's** Kailasanatha patronage - corroboration, but eulogistic.
- **UNESCO 1983.** 📰 The official property page consulted on 29 September 2026 records a three-phase chronology from the 6th to the 12th century, ASI management under the 1958 Act, and integrity threats including seepage, cracking and visitor management - a recorded difference from the core account's 5th-to-11th-century range.

## 14. Udayagiri and Khandagiri

- Odisha, twin hills near **Bhubaneswar**; **more than 30** caves in sandstone, some man-made and some natural.
- **Kharavela**, **Maha-Meghavahana** dynasty, **1st century BC**, a Jain; rock-cutting continued to the **Somavamsis** (**9th-12th century AD**). Udayagiri about **18** caves on the **Kumari** range; Khandagiri about **15**.
- **Rani Gupha**: largest, double-storeyed; **victory-march** relief; **Yavana warrior** sculptures. **Ganesh Gumpha**; **Mancapuri Gumpha**, double-storeyed, **three inscriptions**, one on Kharavela's lineage.
- **Cave 14 Hathigumpha**: a large **natural** cave with the **seventeen-line** inscription in **Prakrit** and **Brahmi**, the **only** evidence about Kharavela; opens with the **Namokar mantra**; three themes - military achievements, benevolent activities, patronage of Jainism; describes him as an **upasaka** respecting all sects (**pasandas**); **omits the Mauryan invasion of Kalinga**.
- Khandagiri: **Trushula Gumpha** (24 Tirthankaras, **Rishab Dev** prominent), **Totowa Gumpha** (guards, bulls, lions, parrots), **Anant Gumpha** (largest there; women, elephants, athletes, geese).
- Recorded comparison: Udayagiri's shrines are more beautiful and better maintained than Khandagiri's. **Not a UNESCO site.**

## 15. Jain heritage beyond the caves

- Three scales: excavated cell; dressed-stone or marble temple; temple-city on a sacred hill.
- **Dilwara** (Mount Abu, Rajasthan) and **Ranakpur** (Rajasthan) - named structural temples. ❌ No dates, patrons, architects, style names or dimensions.
- **Shravanabelagola** (Karnataka): **Bhadrabahu**, pioneer of the **Digambara** sect; **Mahamastakabhisheka** once in **12 years**; the **57 ft** statue of **Siddha Bahubali, son of Rishabhdev**, bathed in milk, sugarcane juice and saffron paste with sandalwood, turmeric and vermilion; the best Jain wall paintings, of the **Nayaka period**.
- **Palitana / Shatrunjaya** (Gujarat), the holiest Jain **tirtha**, associated with **Rishabhanatha**; **Shikharji / Parasnath Hill** (Jharkhand); **Girnar** (Gujarat). ❌ No temple counts.
- **Badami Cave 4**: tirthankaras, with **Parsvanath, Mahavir, Bahubali**. **Ellora 30-34**: Digambara.
- **Khajuraho** holds Hindu and Jain temples sharing one artwork vocabulary - the shared-idiom limitation.
- ❌ Recorded trap: the Shravanabelagola figure is **not** a tirthankara and not the last tirthankara.

## 16. Gupta-age caves and the widening field

- Caves continue; **mural painting** becomes an added feature; **stupa building declines**, with **Dhamek at Sarnath** a renovation; **temple architecture** emerges - square sanctum, pillared portico; **first stage** flat-roofed, square, portico on shallow pillars, low platform: **Temple 17 at Sanchi**.
- **Bagh**: bank of the **Baghni river**, **Dhar district, MP**; **9 Buddhist caves**, c. 5th-6th century; mostly viharas; interior murals; **Cave 4 Rang Mahal**; **Hallisalasya** dance depicted.
- **Udayagiri near Vidisha**: early 5th century, **Chandragupta II**, ~20 caves; **Cave 5 Varaha**, **Cave 6 Sankanika** with an early **Ganesha**, **Cave 7 Tawa** made by **Virasena**, **Cave 13** reclining Vishnu, **Cave 1 Suraj-Gupha** the earliest Hindu temple phase; inscriptions dated **Gupta Samvat 82 (401-02 AD)** and **106 (425-26 AD)**.
- **Junagadh** (Gujarat): **Khapra Kodiya, Baba Pyare, Uparkot**; a **30-50 ft** citadel, **"Upar Kot"**.
- **Mandapeshwar / Montperir** (Borivali): late Gupta **Brahmanical** cave **later converted** into a Christian cave; Nataraja, Sadashiva, Ardhanarishvara.
- **Undavalli** (Guntur, AP, overlooking the Krishna): **four-storeyed** monolithic rock-cut architecture, perhaps 4th-5th centuries; first a **Jain** abode, later **Vishnukundina** (independent c. **429 AD**, capitals at Amaravati then Vijayawada); principal deity **Anantashayana Vishnu**.
- **Elephanta / Gharapuri**, c. **11 km east of Mumbai**: **5 Hindu caves**, Buddhist stupa mounds, **2 Buddhist caves**; **5th-8th centuries**; **attribution debated**; **Portuguese** naming after a **basalt elephant**; **Trimurti / Sadashiva / Maheshmurti** in Cave 1, with Nataraja, Yogishvara, Kalyanasundara, Ravana shaking Kailasa, Gangadhara, Ardhanarishvara; **UNESCO 1987**.

## 17. Answer architecture

- Four qualified theses: rock-cut-as-source; accretion; qualified coexistence; category-limits.
- Mark-scaled structures for 10, 15, Jain 15 and 20 marks, as set out in Lesson 18.
- Three verdict scaffolds: durability and its bias; the same rock repeatedly found sacred; elaboration around an unchanged core.
- Four comparisons that must not collapse: Sanchi-Bharhut; Ellora-Udayagiri/Khandagiri; Barabar-Ajanta; Buddhist-Jain excavated programmes.
- The factual-risk control list of Lesson 18 binds every answer written from this topic.

Both are stated in Lesson 11 and function is used as the constant |
| Ellora's date range | ⚠️ Recorded discrepancy. The core account gives roughly the 5th to 11th centuries AD; the official property page gives a three-phase chronology from the 6th to the 12th century. Both are stated in Lesson 14 |
| The 2026 objective item's question number | ⚠️ Recorded discrepancy. The routing ledger records question 14; the core owner's integration block records question 13; the local scan's degraded numbering places it adjacent to item 15. The ledger is followed and the discrepancy is reported |
| Routing of the 2020 Mains demand | ⚠️ Recorded discrepancy. The Mains ledger routes it to the advanced owner; the core owner records itself as owner of record. Both owners were taught, so the difference is administrative |
| The Didarganj Yakshi's date | ⚠️ Preserved as debated, in line with both owners |
| Elephanta's patron dynasty | ⚠️ Preserved as debated; the source states that scholars attribute the caves to various Hindu dynasties |
| The cause of the decline of Mauryan polish | ⚠️ Preserved as one recorded explanation - high cost - and not as a demonstrated sole cause |
| Ajanta's cave total | ⚠️ The source's figure of 29 is stated; the count varies with what is counted, and the argument is built on the two-campaign division |
| The Rampurva bull pillar's lack of an inscription | ⚠️ Recorded as an absence with no reason supplied, because the source supplies none |

## Where the bounded base was deliberately not extended

⚠️ Three areas were left to their owning topics rather than researched further: the sculptural analysis of the Gandhara, Mathura and Amaravati schools and of the movement from symbol to image; the art-historical analysis of the Ajanta, Bagh and Sittanavasal painting traditions; and conservation policy, protected-monument counts and repatriation questions. ⚠️ Each is named at the point in the lessons where a reader might expect it, so that the boundary is visible rather than silent.
